Regularity results for a class of mixed local and nonlocal singular problems involving distance function
Kaushik Bal and Stuti Das

Abstract
We investigate the following mixed local and nonlocal quasilinear equation with singularity given by − Δ p ​ u + ( − Δ ) q s ​ u = f ⁡ ( x ) u δ ​ in ​ Ω , u > 0 ​ in ​ Ω , u = 0 ​ in ​ ℝ n \ Ω ; \displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u&=\frac{f(x)}{u^{\delta}}\text{ in }\Omega,\\ u&>0\text{ in }\Omega,\\ u&=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega;\end{split} where, ( − Δ ) q s ​ u ​ ( x ) := c n , s ​ P . V . ​ ∫ ℝ n | u ⁡ ( x ) − u ⁡ ( y ) | q − 2 ​ ( u ⁡ ( x ) − u ⁡ ( y ) ) | x − y | n + s ​ q ​ 𝑑 y , (-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u(x):=c_{n,s}\operatorname{P.V.}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy, with Ω \displaystyle\Omega being a bounded domain in ℝ n \displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n} with C 2 \displaystyle C^{2} boundary, 1 < q ≤ p < ∞ \displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty , s ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \displaystyle s\in(0,1) , δ > 0 \displaystyle\delta>0 and f ∈ L loc ∞ ​ ( Ω ) \displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega) is a non-negative function which behaves like 𝐝𝐢𝐬𝐭 ​ ( 𝐱 , ∂ 𝛀 ) − β \displaystyle\mathbf{dist(x,\partial\Omega)^{-\beta}} , β ≥ 0 \displaystyle\beta\geq 0 near ∂ Ω \displaystyle\partial\Omega . We start by proving several Hölder and gradient Hölder regularity results for a more general class of quasilinear operators when δ = 0 \displaystyle\delta=0 . Using the regularity results we deduce existence, uniqueness and Hölder regularity of a weak solution of the singular problem in W loc 1 , p ​ ( Ω ) \displaystyle W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega) and its behavior near ∂ Ω \displaystyle\partial\Omega albeit with different exponents depending on β + δ \displaystyle\beta+\delta . Boundedness and Hölder regularity result to the singular equation with critical exponent were also discussed.



2020 MSC
35J75, 35M10, 35R11.

1
Introduction

The goal of this article is to study a particular class of mixed local-nonlocal problems where mixed refer to a combination of two operators with different orders of derivative, the simplest model being
\displaystyle-\Delta+(-\Delta)^{s}
, for
\displaystyle s\in(0,1)
. More precisely, we aim to we study Hölder regularity results related to the quasilinear operator
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}
,
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty
,
\displaystyle s\in(0,1)
and the existence, uniqueness and regularity of the weak solutions to the following prototype singular problem:

\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}u

\displaystyle=f(x)u^{-\delta},\quad u>0\text{ in }\Omega,

(1.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega;

where
\displaystyle\Omega
is a bounded domain in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\displaystyle C^{2}
boundary
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
and
\displaystyle\delta>0
with
\displaystyle f
behaving like distance function near the boundary.
The motivation behind the study is the apparent lack of homogeneity of the operator in question which makes replicating the results available in the literature to the mixed local-nonlocal cases difficult to reproduce and in some cases false even when
\displaystyle p=q=2
.

We start our discussion by considering the following general form of the operator involving
Eq.

1.1
, given by

{}\mathcal{L}u:=\mathcal{L}_{L}u+\mathcal{L}_{N}u=g\quad\text{ in }\Omega,

(1.2)

where

\mathcal{L}_{L}u(x)=\mathcal{L}_{L}^{A}u(x):=-\operatorname{div}A(x,\nabla u(x))

and

\mathcal{L}_{N}u(x)=\mathcal{L}_{N}^{\Phi,B,s,q}u(x):=\text{ P.V. }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Phi(u(x)-u(y))\frac{B(x,y)}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy.

Also
\displaystyle g\in L^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, for
\displaystyle m\geq n
.
\displaystyle A:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a continuous vector field such that
\displaystyle A(x,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\displaystyle x\in\Omega,A(\cdot,z)\in C^{\bar{\alpha}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and satisfies the following
\displaystyle p
-growth and coercivity conditions

\begin{cases}|A(x,z)|+|z|\left|\partial_{z}A(x,z)\right|\leq\Lambda\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|z|&\text{ for }x\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\\
|A(x,z)-A(y,z)|\leq\Lambda\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|x-y|^{\bar{\alpha}}&\text{ for }x,y\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\left\langle\partial_{z}A(x,z)\xi,\xi\right\rangle\geq\Lambda^{-1}\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi|^{2}&\text{ for }x\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{cases}

for some constants
\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1),\mu\in[0,1]
,
\displaystyle\Lambda\geq 1
, while
\displaystyle B:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a measurable function satisfying

B(x,y)=B(y,x)\quad\text{ and }\quad\Lambda^{-1}\leq B(x,y)\leq\Lambda\quad\text{ for a.e. }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\displaystyle\Phi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R})
is an odd, non-decreasing function fulfilling the
\displaystyle q
-growth and coercivity condition

\Lambda^{-1}|t|^{q}\leq\phi(t)t\leq\Lambda|t|^{q}\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}\text{. }

In this context, we first mention the work in
[
7
]
, where the authors have considered the particular operator
\displaystyle-\Delta+(-\Delta)^{s}
(i.e.
\displaystyle p=q=2,A(x,z)=z,B(x,y)=1,\Phi(t)=t
) under homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, and obtained several results regarding existence, uniqueness, maximum principles, higher summability of solutions depending on the summability of the source term
\displaystyle g
. Further, in this particular case, the same authors, in their paper
[
13
]
, have obtained symmetry results for sufficiently regular solutions. The Brezis-Nirenberg problem in this context was discussed in
[
6
]
. For the quasilinear case
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}+(-\Delta)^{s}_{p}
with Dirichlet boundary data, we refer
[
10
,
11
]
, where necessary and sufficient conditions for the
existence of a unique positive weak solution for some sublinear problems have been obtained. The authors, in
[
11
]
, have also studied the eigenvalue problem for the mixed operator. For more insights into the mixed operator, one may see [
8
,
9
] and the references therein.

Concerning the regularity of weak solutions, we would like to mention
[
24
,
32
,
33
]
, where various local Hölder regularity of solutions (defined in a suitable sense) and their gradients have been obtained. In this regard, the novelty of our work is to consider the solutions in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
along with
\displaystyle g\neq 0
and a combination of non-homogeneous
\displaystyle(p,q)
operator. We first show any solution
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to the equation
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}u=g
is indeed locally almost Lipschitz continuous provided
\displaystyle g\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
. Next, for the same range of
\displaystyle p
and
\displaystyle q
, we improve the almost Lipschitz regularity result for the general form of the equation given by
Eq.

1.2
and
\displaystyle g\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
along with a local gradient Hölder regularity.

For regularity results up to the boundary, we refer
[
1
,
45
,
46
]
. Following the same perturbation approach used in
[
1
]
, we prove that if a non-negative solution
\displaystyle u
to
Eq.

1.2
behaves like distance function near the boundary, then it is in
\displaystyle C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
, for some
\displaystyle\alpha\in(0,1)
, when
\displaystyle 0\leq g\leq Cd(x)^{-\sigma}
for
\displaystyle\sigma<1
and
\displaystyle d(x):=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
.

As far as the singular elliptic problems are concerned, we would like to start by mentioning the now classical work of Crandall-Rabinowitz-Tartar
[
22
]
, who showed that the problem given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle{}\begin{split}-\Delta u&={f}u^{-\delta}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad u>0\;\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u&=0\;\text{ in }\partial\Omega;\end{split}

admits a unique solution
\displaystyle u\in C^{2}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega})
for any
\displaystyle\delta>0
along with the fact that the solution must behave like a distance function near the boundary provided
\displaystyle f
is Hölder Continuous. There has been an extensive study in this direction since this pioneering work, including both local and nonlocal cases, see for instance
[
5
,
14
,
19
,
20
,
36
,
39
,
40
]
and the references therein.

Turning to the mixed local-nonlocal singular problems, we refer to the works of [
3
,
4
,
12
,
30
,
31
,
34
], where several results regarding existence, summability, multiplicity have been obtained. Inspired by the above discussion, in this work, we consider singular problems given by
Eq.

1.1
, assuming that
\displaystyle f\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
satisfies the condition

\displaystyle c_{1}d(x)^{-\beta}\leq f(x)\leq c_{2}d(x)^{-\beta}\text{ in }\Omega_{\rho},

where
\displaystyle c_{1},c_{2}
are nonnegative constants,
\displaystyle\beta\geq 0
and
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}:=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}:d(x)<\rho\}
for
\displaystyle\rho>0
. We obtain existence, uniqueness, boundary behavior, optimal Sobolev regularity and most importantly, up to the boundary Hölder regularity of the problem
Eq.

1.1
. To prove the existence of a weak solution, we use the perturbation argument used in
[
14
]
. We prove suitable uniform boundedness of the sequence of approximated solutions in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
(or in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega))
and pass through the limit. While proving the boundary behavior of solutions, due to the nonhomogeneous nature of the leading operator, sub-super solution methods as previously used, cannot be implemented in our case. We overcome this difficulty by exploiting
\displaystyle C^{2}
regularity of
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
which guarantees that the distance function is
\displaystyle C^{2}
in some neighborhood of the boundary in combination with the barrier functions as done in
[
2
]
and using the property that their fractional Laplacian gives a
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
function only in a neighborhood of
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
as done in
[
35
]
.

The behavior, as mentioned above, of the solution near the boundary allows us to establish the optimal Sobolev regularity for the weak solution to
Eq.

1.1
and a non-existence result for the case
\displaystyle\beta>p
. Moreover, we prove a comparison principle for sub and super solution of
Eq.

1.1
in
\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
for the case of
\displaystyle\beta<2-1/p
.

Summarizing, we observe that the leading operator in problem
Eq.

1.1
can be non-homogeneous in nature, that is, there does not exist any
\displaystyle\alpha>0
such that
\displaystyle K(t\xi)=t^{\alpha}K(\xi)
for all
\displaystyle\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle t>0
, where
\displaystyle K(\xi):=-\Delta_{p}\xi+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}\xi
and the nonlinear term is doubly singular in the sense that it involves two singular terms,
\displaystyle u^{-\delta}
and the weight function which blows up near the boundary. Thus, the novelty of the paper lies in the following:

(i) We consider a class of nonhomogeneous mixed local-nonlocal operators with different orders of exponents and establish almost optimal global Hölder continuity results for problems involving singular nonlinearities.

(ii) We prove behavior of the solution to
Eq.

1.1
near the boundary where the standard scaling argument fails.

(iii) The weak comparison principle where the source term is doubly nonlinear. Here, the sub and super solutions of the equation may not be in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. A non-existence result for the case
\displaystyle\beta>p
has also been obtained.

(iv) Several Hölder and gradient Hölder regularity results, which are of independent interest, have been obtained in a completely new set-up where we allow solutions to be in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We obtain a completely new Hölder continuity for the gradient of weak solution of some quasilinear equation involving lower order terms with nonhomogeneous nature and singular nonlinearity. This gives us
\displaystyle C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
regularity for the case
\displaystyle\beta+\delta<1
.

(v) We prove almost Lipschitz regularity up to the boundary for the case
\displaystyle\beta+\delta=1
. Our method does not require solutions to lie in the energy space to prove Hölder continuity results when
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\geq 1
. Also, a local Hölder regularity for the gradient is obtained for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\geq 1
.

(vi) We have obtained an a priori
\displaystyle C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
regularity for solutions to
Eq.

1.1
under a perturbation
\displaystyle b(x,u)
, when
\displaystyle\delta<1,\beta=0
and
\displaystyle 0\leq b(x,u)\leq c(1+|u|^{p^{*}-1})
.

We finally remark that, after a first draft of this article was posted on Arxiv, a related preprint
[
25
]
appeared,
where similar results was obtained for
\displaystyle qs\leq p
. It is worth mentioning that our regularity results involves a weaker notion of solutions and gives better Hölder regularity in relation to the singular problems along with better boundary behaviors.

Layout of the paper:
In
Section

2
, we will write notations and give definitions and embedding results regarding Sobolev spaces as required. Appropriate notions of weak solutions for our problems will be defined, and the main results will be stated.
Section

3
contains proof of almost Lipschitz regularity (locally) for
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u=g
in
\displaystyle\Omega
where the solutions belong to
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\displaystyle g\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
; and
Section

4
extends this for the equation
Eq.

1.2
along with
\displaystyle g\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. Higher Hölder regularity for
Eq.

1.2
is obtained in
Section

5
whereas
Section

6
gives up to the boundary gradient Hölder regularity when the source term can be nonlinear in the sense that it blows up near the boundary. Finally,
Section

7
contains the singular problem
Eq.

1.1
and all the proofs regarding this. We end with
Section

8
, which contains an application to a perturbed problem by obtaining the
\displaystyle C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
regularity.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

\displaystyle\bullet
We will take
\displaystyle n
to be the space dimension and
\displaystyle\Omega
to be a bounded domain in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\displaystyle C^{2}
boundary.

\displaystyle\bullet
The Lebesgue measure of a measurable subset
\displaystyle\mathrm{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be denoted by
\displaystyle|\mathrm{S}|
.

\displaystyle\bullet
For
\displaystyle r>1
, the Hölder conjugate exponent of
\displaystyle r
will be denoted by
\displaystyle r^{\prime}=\frac{r}{r-1}
.

\displaystyle\bullet
For any open subset
\displaystyle\Omega
of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\displaystyle K\subset\subset\Omega
will imply
\displaystyle K
is compactly contained in
\displaystyle\Omega.

\displaystyle\bullet

\displaystyle\int
will denote integration concerning space only, and integration on
\displaystyle\Omega\times\Omega
or
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be denoted by a double integral
\displaystyle\iint
. Moreover, the average integral will be denoted by
\displaystyle\fint
.

\displaystyle\bullet
The notation
\displaystyle a\lesssim b
will be used for
\displaystyle a\leq Cb
, where
\displaystyle C
is a universal constant that only depends on the prescribed data.
\displaystyle C
(or
\displaystyle c
) may vary from line to line or even in the same line.

\displaystyle\bullet
For a function
\displaystyle h
, we denote its positive and negative parts by
\displaystyle h^{+}=\max\{h,0\}
,
\displaystyle h^{-}=\max\{-h,0\}
respectively.

\displaystyle\bullet
For
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote
\displaystyle T_{k}(\sigma)=\max\{-k,\min\{k,\sigma\}\}
, for
\displaystyle\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.2
Function Spaces

We recall that for
\displaystyle E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Lebesgue space

\displaystyle L^{p}(E),1\leq p<\infty
, is defined to be the space of
\displaystyle p
-integrable functions
\displaystyle u:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with the finite norm

\|u\|_{L^{p}(E)}=\left(\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}.

By
\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(E)
we denote the space of locally
\displaystyle p
-integrable functions, i.e.
\displaystyle u\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{p}(E)
if and only if
\displaystyle u\in L^{p}(F)
for every
\displaystyle F\subset\subset E
. In the case
\displaystyle 0<p<1
, we denote by
\displaystyle L^{p}(E)
a set of measurable functions such that
\displaystyle\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}dx<\infty
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open set with
\displaystyle C^{1}
boundary. The Sobolev space
\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, for
\displaystyle 1\leq p<\infty
is defined as the Banach space of all integrable and weakly differentiable functions
\displaystyle u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
equipped with the following norm

\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}=\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

The space
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
is defined as the closure of the space
\displaystyle{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, in the norm of the Sobolev space
\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, where
\displaystyle{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is the set of all smooth functions whose supports are compactly contained in
\displaystyle\Omega
. As
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
is
\displaystyle C^{1}
, by [
17
, Proposition 9.18],
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
can be identified by
\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{p}(\Omega)=\{u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):u=0\text{ a.e. in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega\}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\displaystyle 0<s<1
and
\displaystyle\Omega
be an open connected subset of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\displaystyle C^{1}
boundary. The fractional Sobolev space
\displaystyle W^{s,q}(\Omega)
for any
\displaystyle 1\leq q<\infty
is defined as

W^{s,q}(\Omega)=\left\{u\in L^{q}(\Omega):\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\frac{n}{q}+s}}\in L^{q}(\Omega\times\Omega)\right\},

and it is endowed with the norm

{}\|u\|_{W^{s,q}(\Omega)}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{q}dx+\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{1/q}.

(2.1)

It can be treated as an intermediate space between
\displaystyle W^{1,q}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle L^{q}(\Omega)
. For
\displaystyle 0<s_{1}\leq s_{2}<1
,
\displaystyle W^{s_{2},q}(\Omega)
is continuously embedded in
\displaystyle W^{s_{1},q}(\Omega)
, see [
26
, Proposition 2.1]. The fractional Sobolev space with zero boundary values is defined by

W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)=\left\{u\in W^{s,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):u=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega\right\}.

However if
\displaystyle sq\neq 1
,
\displaystyle W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)
can be treated as the closure of
\displaystyle{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
in
\displaystyle W^{s,q}(\Omega)
with respect to the fractional Sobolev norm defined in
Eq.

2.1
. Both
\displaystyle W^{s,q}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)
are reflexive Banach spaces, for
\displaystyle q>1
, for details we refer to the readers [
26
, Section 2]. The spaces
\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
are also reflexive for
\displaystyle p>1
.

The following result asserts that the classical Sobolev space is continuously embedded in the fractional Sobolev space; see [
26
, Proposition 2.2]. The idea applies an extension property of
\displaystyle\Omega
so that we can extend functions from
\displaystyle W^{1,q}(\Omega)
to
\displaystyle W^{1,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and that the extension operator is bounded.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\displaystyle C^{1}
boundary. Then there exists
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,\Omega)>0
such that

\|u\|_{W^{s,q}(\Omega)}\leq c\|u\|_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)},

for every
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,q}(\Omega)
.

The next embedding result follows from [
18
, Lemma 2.1] for Sobolev spaces with zero boundary value. The fundamental difference of it compared to
Lemma

2.3
is that the result holds for any bounded domain (without any smoothness condition on the boundary), since for the Sobolev spaces with zero boundary value, we always have a zero extension to the complement.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\displaystyle 0<s<1
. Then there exists
\displaystyle C=C(n,s,\Omega)>0
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{q}dx,

for every
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,q}(\Omega)
. Here, we consider the zero extension of
\displaystyle u
to the complement of
\displaystyle\Omega
.

One particular version of
Lemma

2.4
can be found in [
24
, Lemma 2.2].

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\displaystyle 1\leq q\leq p<\infty,s\in(0,1),B_{\varrho}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball. If
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(B_{\varrho})
, then
\displaystyle u\in W^{s,q}(B_{\varrho})
and

\left(\int_{B_{\varrho}}\fint_{B_{\varrho}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{1/q}\leq c\varrho^{1-s}\left(\fint_{B_{\varrho}}|\nabla u|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}

holds with
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,p,q,s)
.

The following useful lemma can be found in [
24
, Lemma 3.2].

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\displaystyle s\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle w\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\displaystyle B_{\varrho}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball. Then there exists
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,q,s)
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{\varrho}}\frac{|w(y)|^{q-1}}{\left|y-x_{0}\right|^{n+sq}}dy\leq\frac{c}{\varrho^{s}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{\varrho}}\frac{|w(y)|^{q}}{\left|y-x_{0}\right|^{n+sq}}dy\right)^{1-1/q}.

We now proceed with the essential Poincaré inequality, which can be found in [
28
, Chapter 5, Section 5.8.1].

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with
\displaystyle{C}^{1}
boundary. Then there exists
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,\Omega)>0
, such that

\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}dx\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}dx,\qquad\forall u\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega).

Specifically if we take
\displaystyle\Omega=B_{\bar{r}}
, then we will get for all
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}(B_{\bar{r}})
,

\fint_{B_{\bar{r}}}\left|u-(u)_{B_{\bar{r}}}\right|^{p}dx\leq c{\bar{r}}^{p}\fint_{B_{\bar{r}}}|\nabla u|^{p}dx,

where
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n)>0
and
\displaystyle(u)_{B_{\bar{r}}}
denotes the average of
\displaystyle u
in
\displaystyle B_{\bar{r}}
, and
\displaystyle B_{\bar{r}}
is a ball of radius
\displaystyle{\bar{r}}
centered at
\displaystyle x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Using
Lemma

2.4
and
Lemma

2.7
, we observe that the following norm on the space
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
defined by

\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+sp}}dxdy\right)^{1/p},

is equivalent to the norm

\|u\|_{W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}.

The following is a version of fractional Poincaré.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\displaystyle\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with
\displaystyle{C}^{1}
boundary,
\displaystyle s\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle q\geq 1
. If
\displaystyle u\in W^{s,q}_{0}(\Omega)
, then

\int_{\Omega}|u|^{q}dx\leq c\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy,

holds with
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,\Omega)
.

In view of
Lemma

2.8
, we observe that the Banach space
\displaystyle W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)
can be endowed with the norm

\|u\|_{W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)}=\left(\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{1/q},

which is equivalent to that of
\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{s,q}(\Omega)}
.
Now, we define the local spaces as

W_{\operatorname{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)=\left\{u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:u\in L^{p}(K),\int_{K}|\nabla u|^{p}dx<\infty,\text{ for every }K\subset\subset\Omega\right\},

and

W_{\operatorname{loc}}^{s,q}(\Omega)=\left\{u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:u\in L^{q}(K),\int_{K}\int_{K}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy<\infty,\text{ for every }K\subset\subset\Omega\right\}.

Now for
\displaystyle n>p
, we define the critical Sobolev exponent as
\displaystyle p^{*}=\frac{np}{n-p}
, then we get the following embedding result for any bounded open subset
\displaystyle\Omega
of class
\displaystyle{C}^{1}
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
, see for details [
28
, Chapter 5].

Theorem 2.9
.

The embedding operators

\displaystyle\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\begin{cases}L^{t}(\Omega),&\text{ for }t\in\left[1,p^{*}\right],\text{ if }p\in(1,n),\\
L^{t}(\Omega),&\text{ for }t\in[1,\infty),\text{ if }p=n,\\
L^{\infty}(\Omega),&\text{ if }p>n,\end{cases}

are continuous. Also, the above embeddings are compact, except for
\displaystyle t=p^{*}
, if
\displaystyle p\in(1,n)
.

Similarly, for
\displaystyle n>qs
, we define the fractional Sobolev critical exponent as
\displaystyle q_{s}^{*}=\frac{nq}{n-qs}
. The following result is a fractional version of the Sobolev inequality (
Theorem

2.9
), which also implies a continuous embedding of
\displaystyle W_{0}^{s,q}(\Omega)
in the critical Lebesgue space
\displaystyle L^{q_{s}^{*}}(\Omega)
. One can see the proof in
[
26
]
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\displaystyle 0<s<1
be such that
\displaystyle n>qs
. Then, there exists a constant
\displaystyle S(n,s)
depending only on
\displaystyle n
and
\displaystyle s
, such that for all
\displaystyle u\in{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)

\|u\|_{L^{q_{s}^{*}}(\Omega)}\leq S(n,s)\left(\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{1/q}.

The following version of Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality will be useful for us, see [
43
, Corollary 1.57].

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
\displaystyle 1<p<\infty,\Omega
be an open set in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\displaystyle|\Omega|<\infty
and

{}\kappa=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{n-p},&\text{ if }1<p<n,\\
2,&\text{ if }p\geq n.\end{cases}

(2.2)

There exists a positive constant
\displaystyle C=C(n,p)
such that for every
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
, it holds

\left(\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{\kappa p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa p}}\leq C|\Omega|^{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{\kappa p}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

For the following theorem, see
[
21
]
.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
\displaystyle U
be a nonempty closed convex subset of a real separable reflexive Banach space
\displaystyle V
and let
\displaystyle A:V\rightarrow V^{\prime}
be a coercive demicontinuous monotone operator. Then for every
\displaystyle f\in V^{\prime}
, there exists
\displaystyle u\in U
so that

\langle A(u),v-u\rangle\geq\langle f,v-u\rangle\text{ for all }v\in U.

Moreover, if
\displaystyle A
is strictly monotone, then
\displaystyle u
is unique.

See [
19
, Lemma 3.5] for the following result.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\displaystyle m>1
and
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
. For
\displaystyle(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let us set

S_{\epsilon}^{x}:=\{x\geq\epsilon\}\cap\{y\geq 0\}\text{ and }S_{\epsilon}^{y}=\{y\geq\epsilon\}\cap\{x\geq 0\}.

Then for every
\displaystyle(x,y)\in S_{\epsilon}^{x}\cup S_{\epsilon}^{y}
, we have

\epsilon^{m-1}|x-y|\leq|x^{m}-y^{m}|.

Next, we state the algebraic inequality from [
23
, Lemma 2.1].

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\displaystyle 1<p<\infty
. Then for any
\displaystyle a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exists a constant
\displaystyle C\equiv C(p)>0
, such that

\langle|a|^{p-2}a-|b|^{p-2}b\ ,a-b\rangle\geq C\frac{|a-b|^{2}}{(|a|+|b|)^{2-p}}.

2.3
Weak Solutions and Main Results

In this subsection, we define the notion of a weak solution to different problems and state our main results.

Definition 2.15
.

(Tail Space)
Let
\displaystyle u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function and let
\displaystyle 0<m<\infty
and
\displaystyle\alpha>0
. We define the tail space by

L_{\alpha}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{u\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)|^{m}dx}{(1+|x|^{n+\alpha})}<\infty\right\}.

The nonlocal tail centered at
\displaystyle x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with radius
\displaystyle R>0
is defined by

T_{m,\alpha}(u;x_{0},R)=\left(R^{\alpha}\int_{B_{R}(x_{0})^{c}}\frac{|u(y)|^{m}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+\alpha}}dy\right)^{1/m}.

Set
\displaystyle T_{m,\alpha}(u;R)=T_{m,\alpha}(u;0,R)
. We will follow the notation

T_{q-1}(u;x,R):=T_{q-1,sq}(u;x,R),

unless otherwise stated.

Definition 2.16
.

(Local weak solution)
Let
\displaystyle f\in L^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, where
\displaystyle m\geq(p^{*})^{\prime}\text{ if }p<n,m>1\text{ if }p=n\text{ and }m\geq 1\text{ if }p>n
. A function
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is said to be a local weak sub-solution (or super-solution) of

{}-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u=f\text{ in }\Omega,

(2.3)

if for every
\displaystyle K\subset\subset\Omega
and for every non-negative function
\displaystyle\phi\in W_{0}^{1,p}(K)
, we have

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\leq(\text{or}\geq)\int_{\Omega}f\phi\,dx.\end{array}

(2.4)

We say
\displaystyle u
is a weak solution to
Eq.

2.3
if equality holds in
Eq.

2.4
for all
\displaystyle\phi\in W_{0}^{1,p}(K)
.

We now state our first Hölder regularity result for
Eq.

2.3
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Suppose
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. Let

\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

be a local weak solution to
Eq.

2.3
. Then, for every
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1)
,
there holds
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,\sigma}(\Omega)
. Moreover, for every
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle{R}_{0}\in(0,1)
with
\displaystyle B_{2{R}_{0}}(x_{0})\subset\subset\Omega
, the following holds:

[u]_{C^{0,\sigma}(B_{R_{0}/2}(x_{0}))}\leq\frac{C}{R^{\sigma}}\left(1+T_{q-1}(u;x_{0},{R}_{0})+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}))}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}))}\right),

for some
\displaystyle C\equiv C(n,s,p,q,\sigma)>0
.

Now, we state some improved Hölder regularity results for a general class of operators. We consider

{}\mathcal{L}u:=\mathcal{L}_{L}u+\mathcal{L}_{N}u

(2.5)

defined as the sum of the local term

\mathcal{L}_{L}u(x)=\mathcal{L}_{L}^{A}u(x):=-\operatorname{div}A(x,\nabla u(x))

and of the nonlocal one

\mathcal{L}_{N}u(x)=\mathcal{L}_{N}^{\Phi,B,s,q}u(x):=\text{ P.V. }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Phi(u(x)-u(y))\frac{B(x,y)}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy.

Here,
\displaystyle A:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a continuous vector field such that
\displaystyle A(x,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\displaystyle x\in\Omega,A(\cdot,z)\in C^{\bar{\alpha}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and which satisfies the following
\displaystyle p
-growth and coercivity conditions

{}\begin{cases}|A(x,z)|+|z|\left|\partial_{z}A(x,z)\right|\leq\Lambda\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|z|&\text{ for }x\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\\
|A(x,z)-A(y,z)|\leq\Lambda\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}|x-y|^{\bar{\alpha}}&\text{ for }x,y\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\left\langle\partial_{z}A(x,z)\xi,\xi\right\rangle\geq\Lambda^{-1}\left(|z|^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\xi|^{2}&\text{ for }x\in\Omega,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{cases}

(2.6)

for some constants
\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}\in(0,1),\mu\in[0,1]
, and
\displaystyle\Lambda\geq 1
, while
\displaystyle B:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a measurable function satisfying

{}B(x,y)=B(y,x)\quad\text{ and }\quad\Lambda^{-1}\leq B(x,y)\leq\Lambda\quad\text{ for a.e. }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(2.7)

and
\displaystyle\Phi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R})
is an odd, non-decreasing function fulfilling the
\displaystyle q
-growth and coercivity condition

{}\Lambda^{-1}|t|^{q}\leq\phi(t)t\leq\Lambda|t|^{q}\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}\text{. }

(2.8)

In
Section

7
, we will use Hopf lemma, and for that, we will assume
\displaystyle B\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\displaystyle\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})
with

{}|B(x+w,y+z)-B(x,y)|\leq\Lambda(|w|+|z|)\quad\forall x,y,w,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\Lambda^{-1}|t|^{q-2}\leq\phi^{\prime}(t)\leq\Lambda|t|^{q-2}\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}.

(2.9)

A prototypical example of such an operator is, of course

\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+\left(-\Delta_{q}\right)^{s}u,
which is obtained by taking
\displaystyle A(x,z)=|z|^{p-2}z,\phi(t)=|t|^{q-2}t
, and
\displaystyle B
equal to a constant. Our next result is an improved version of
Theorem

2.17
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\displaystyle f\in L^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, where
\displaystyle m\geq(p^{*})^{\prime}\text{ if }p<n,m>1\text{ if }p=n\text{ and }m\geq 1\text{ if }p>n
. A function
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is said to be a local weak sub-solution (or super-solution) of

{}\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega,

(2.10)

if for every
\displaystyle K\subset\subset\Omega
and for every non-negative function
\displaystyle\phi\in W_{0}^{1,p}(K)
, we have

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\Phi(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))B(x,y)}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\leq(\text{or}\geq)\int_{\Omega}f\phi\,dx.\end{array}

(2.11)

We say
\displaystyle u
is a weak solution to
Eq.

2.10
if equality holds in
Eq.

2.11
for all
\displaystyle\phi\in W_{0}^{1,p}(K)
.

Theorem 2.19
.

(a) Let
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
,
\displaystyle f\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

be a local weak solution to

\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega.

Then, for each
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
, one has
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,\eta}(\Omega)
. Moreover, for every
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle{R}_{0}\in(0,1)
with
\displaystyle B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0})\subset\subset\Omega
,

\displaystyle[u]_{C^{0,\eta}(B_{R_{0}/4}(x_{0}))}\leq C

holds for some
\displaystyle C\equiv C(n,s,p,q,\bar{\alpha},\Lambda,\eta,\|u\|_{L^{p}(B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0}))},\|f\|_{L^{n}(B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0}))},Tail_{q-1}(u;x_{0},R_{0}))
.

(b) Suppose
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle f\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. Let

\displaystyle 0\leq u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be such that there exists some
\displaystyle\theta\geq 1
with
\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
If
\displaystyle u
is bounded and is a local weak solution to

\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega,

then, for every
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
,
one has
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,\eta}(\Omega)
. Moreover, for every
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle{R}_{0}\in(0,1)
with
\displaystyle B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0})\subset\subset\Omega
,

\displaystyle[u]_{C^{0,\eta}(B_{R_{0}/4}(x_{0}))}\leq C

holds for some
\displaystyle C\equiv C(n,s,p,q,\bar{\alpha},\Lambda,\eta,\|u\|_{L^{p}(B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0}))},\|f\|_{L^{n}(B_{{R}_{0}}(x_{0}))},\|u^{\theta}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})>0
.

Following is the gradient Hölder regularity for local weak solutions.

Theorem 2.20
.

(a) Suppose
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle f\in L^{d}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle d>n
. Let

\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

be a local weak solution to

\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega.

Then, there exists
\displaystyle\bar{\eta}\in(0,1)
such that
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,\bar{\eta}}(\Omega)
.

(b) Suppose
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle f\in L^{d}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle d>n
. Let

\displaystyle 0\leq u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be such that there exists some
\displaystyle\theta\geq 1
with
\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
If
\displaystyle u
is bounded and is a local weak solution to

\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega,

then, there exists
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
,
such that
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,\gamma}(\Omega)
.

Now, we state the boundary regularity result that has been obtained.

Theorem 2.21
.

Suppose
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle f
satisfies
\displaystyle 0\leq f\leq Cd(x,\partial\Omega)^{-\sigma}
in
\displaystyle\Omega
for some
\displaystyle\sigma\in[0,1)
. Let
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be a solution to the problem

{}\mathcal{L}u=f\text{ in }\Omega;\quad u=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega,

satisfying
\displaystyle 0\leq u\leq M
and
\displaystyle 0\leq u\leq Cd(x,\partial\Omega)^{\epsilon}
a.e. in
\displaystyle\Omega
for some
\displaystyle M,C=C(\epsilon)>0
and for some
\displaystyle\epsilon\in[\sigma,1)
. Then there exists
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
such that
\displaystyle u\in C^{1,\gamma}(\overline{\Omega})
and
\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq c
for some
\displaystyle c
depending on
\displaystyle n,p,q,s,\Lambda,\bar{\alpha},\Omega,M,\sigma
.

We get the following result for a perturbed problem.

Theorem 2.22
.

Suppose
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be a solution to

{}-\Delta_{p}u(x)+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}u(x)=f(x,u(x))\text{ in }\Omega;\quad u=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega,

(2.12)

with
\displaystyle f(x):=f(x,u(x))
is a Carathéodory function satisfying
\displaystyle|f(x,t)|\leq C_{0}(1+|t|^{p^{*}-1})
, where
\displaystyle C_{0}>0
and
\displaystyle p^{*}
is the critical Sobolev exponent if
\displaystyle p<n
and otherwise an arbitrary large number. Then
\displaystyle u\in C^{1,\gamma}(\overline{\Omega})
for some
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
.

We now turn to the singular problem given by

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u&={f(x)}{u^{-\delta}}\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u&>0\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u&=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega;\end{split}\end{array}

(2.13)

where
\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and satisfies the condition

{}c_{1}d(x,\partial\Omega)^{-\beta}\leq f(x)\leq c_{2}d(x,\partial\Omega)^{-\beta},

(2.14)

in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}:d(x,\partial\Omega)<\rho\}
. Also,
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty,s\in(0,1),\delta>0,\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with
\displaystyle C^{2}
boundary. Since
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
is
\displaystyle C^{2}
, it follows from [
37
, Lemma 14.6, Page 355] that there exists
\displaystyle\varrho_{0}>0
such that
\displaystyle d\in C^{2}(\Omega_{\varrho_{0}})
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
\displaystyle\rho\leq\min\left\{\varrho_{0}/2,1\right\}
, so that
\displaystyle|\Delta d|\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})
and
Eq.

2.14
also holds. We define the notion of weak solution to
Eq.

2.13
as follows.

Definition 2.23
.

We say that
\displaystyle u\leq 0
on
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
, if
\displaystyle(u-\epsilon)^{+}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
for every
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and
\displaystyle u=0
on
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
if
\displaystyle u\geq 0
in
\displaystyle\Omega
and
\displaystyle u\leq 0
on
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
.

Definition 2.24
.

A function
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
is said to be a weak sub-solution (resp. supersolution) of problem
Eq.

2.13
if
\displaystyle u>0
in
\displaystyle\Omega
,
\displaystyle{f}{u^{-\delta}}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\Omega)
and the followings hold:

(i) for all
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, with
\displaystyle\phi\geq 0
in
\displaystyle\Omega
,

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\leq(\text{ resp. }\geq)\int_{\Omega}f(x)u^{-\delta}\phi,

(ii) there exists
\displaystyle\gamma\geq 1
such that
\displaystyle u^{\gamma}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. This condition implies that the boundary behavior of
\displaystyle u
is verified according to
Definition

2.23
.

A function which is both sub and super solution of
Eq.

2.13
is called a weak solution.

Remark 2.25
.

By the positivity of
\displaystyle u
and condition
\displaystyle(ii)
above, the corresponding tail term is finite and using
Lemma

2.3
and
Lemma

2.4
, the inequality mentioned in the definition makes sense.

Definition 2.26
.

We say a weak solution
\displaystyle u
of
Eq.

2.13
, is in the conical shell
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{d_{\beta,\delta}}
if it is continuous and satisfies the following

\begin{cases}\eta d(x)\leq u(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\bar{\sigma}}&\text{ if }\beta+\delta\leq 1,\text{ for all }0<\bar{\sigma}<1,\\
\eta d(x)^{\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}}\leq u(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}}&\text{ if }\beta+\delta>1\text{ with }\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}\in(0,s),\\
\eta d(x)\leq u(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}}&\text{ if }\beta+\delta>1\text{ with }\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}\in[s,1),\beta\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta),\\
\eta d(x)\leq u(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\frac{p-\beta_{1}}{p-1+\delta}}&\text{ if }\beta+\delta>1\text{ with }\beta=p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta),\text{ for all }\beta_{1}\in(\beta,p),\end{cases}

for some positive constants
\displaystyle\eta,\Gamma>0
.

Now, we state our existence result.

Theorem 2.27
.

Let
\displaystyle\beta\in[0,p)
, then
Eq.

2.13
admits a weak solution (obtained as a limit of solution to approximated problem
Eq.

7.3
of problem
Eq.

2.13
)
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap\,\mathcal{C}_{d_{\beta,\delta}}
, in the sense of
Definition

2.24
. This existence result (without the fact that
\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{\beta,\delta}}
) also holds if we consider the same singular problems associated with the general operator given by
Eq.

2.5
(considering
\displaystyle\mu=0
in
Eq.

2.6
).

To obtain the uniqueness result, we establish the following weak comparison principle.

Theorem 2.28
.

Let
\displaystyle\beta<2-\frac{1}{p}
and
\displaystyle u,v\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be sub and super solution of
Eq.

2.13
, respectively in the sense of
Definition

2.24
. Then,
\displaystyle u\leq v
a.e. in
\displaystyle\Omega
. Again, this uniqueness result holds if we consider singular problems associated with the general operator given by
Eq.

2.5
(considering
\displaystyle\mu=0
in
Eq.

2.6
).

Next follows the optimal Sobolev regularity obtained.

Theorem 2.29
.

Let
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, and take
\displaystyle\Lambda=\frac{(p-1)(p-1+\delta)}{p(p-\beta)}
. If
\displaystyle{p}<\beta+s(p+\delta-1)
then we have weak solution
\displaystyle u
to
Eq.

2.13
, obtained as the limit of approximating sequence belongs to
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
iff
\displaystyle\Lambda<1
i.e. iff
\displaystyle\delta<2+\frac{1-\beta p}{p-1}
and

\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
iff
\displaystyle\theta>\Lambda\geq 1
. Further for
\displaystyle{p}\geq\beta+s(p+\delta-1)
, define
\displaystyle\Lambda=\frac{(p-1)(p-1+\delta)}{p(p-\beta)}
if
\displaystyle\beta\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta)
. Then the weak solution
\displaystyle u
to
Eq.

2.13
belongs to
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
if
\displaystyle\Lambda<1
and

\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
if
\displaystyle\theta>\Lambda\geq 1
.

Next, we have a non-existence result for weak solution of
Eq.

2.13
.

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
\displaystyle\beta\geq p
in
Eq.

2.14
. Then, there does not exist any weak solution of problem
Eq.

2.13
in the sense of
Definition

2.24
.

Finally, we prove the following almost optimal global regularity result in the singular setting.

Theorem 2.31
.

Suppose
\displaystyle\beta\in[0,p)
and
\displaystyle\delta>0
. Let
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be either the solution given by
Theorem

2.27
or the unique solution to problem
Eq.

2.13
. Then the following assertions hold:

(i) If
\displaystyle\beta+\delta<1
, then
\displaystyle u\in C^{1,\sigma}(\overline{\Omega})
for some
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1)
.

(ii) If
\displaystyle\beta+\delta=1
, then
\displaystyle u\in C^{0,\eta}(\overline{\Omega})
for all
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1)
.

(iii) If
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
with
\displaystyle\beta\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta)
, then

u\in C^{0,\frac{p-\beta}{p-1+\delta}}(\overline{\Omega}).

(iv) If
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
with
\displaystyle\beta=p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta)
, then

u\in C^{0,\frac{p-\beta_{1}}{p-1+\delta}}(\overline{\Omega}),\quad\text{ for all }\beta_{1}\in\left(\beta,p\right).

Also, in the cases (ii), (iii) and (iv),
\displaystyle u\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,\gamma}(\overline{\Omega})
for some
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
.

As an application of the above results, we deduce the following corollary (compare this with
Theorem

2.22
) for critical growth problems.

Corollary 2.32
.

Let
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be a weak solution to the problem

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{split}-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u&=\frac{\lambda}{u^{\delta}}+b(x,u)\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u&>0\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u&=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega;\end{split}\end{array}

(2.15)

where
\displaystyle\delta\in(0,1),\lambda>0
is a parameter and
\displaystyle b:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a Carathéodory function satisfying

0\leq b(x,t)\leq C_{b}(1+|t|^{p^{*}-1}),

with
\displaystyle C_{b}>0
as a constant. Then
\displaystyle u\in C^{1,\sigma}(\overline{\Omega})
for some
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1)
.

3
Regularity results for regular problems

The following energy estimate will be crucial for us. Let us denote an open ball with center
\displaystyle x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and radius
\displaystyle r>0
by
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty,f\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q-1}_{sq}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a local weak subsolution of
Eq.

2.3
and denote
\displaystyle w=(u-k)^{+}
with
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{R}
. There exists a positive constant
\displaystyle C=C(p,q,n,s)
such that

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}|\nabla w|^{p}dx+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|w(x)\psi^{p/q}(x)-w(y)\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\\
\leq C\left(\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}w^{p}|\nabla\psi|^{p}dx+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\max\{w(x),w(y)\}^{q}|\psi^{p/q}(x)-\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\right.\\
\left.\quad+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{w(y)^{q-1}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy\right)w\psi^{p}dx+r^{-sq}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}w^{q}dx\right)+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|f|w\psi^{p}dx,\end{array}

(3.1)

whenever
\displaystyle B_{r}\left(x_{0}\right)\subset\Omega
and
\displaystyle\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))
is a nonnegative function. We have denoted
\displaystyle d\mu=\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy
. If
\displaystyle u
is a weak supersolution of
Eq.

2.3
, the estimate in
Eq.

3.1
holds with
\displaystyle w=(u-k)^{-}
.

Proof.

For
\displaystyle w=(u-k)^{+}
, we choose
\displaystyle\phi=w\psi^{p}
as a test function in
Eq.

2.4
, to obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|f|w\psi^{p}dx\geq\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}fw\psi^{p}dx&\geq&\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\left(w\psi^{p}\right)dx\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(w(x)\psi(x)^{p}-w(y)\psi(y)^{p})d\mu\\
&=&I+J.\end{array}

(3.2)

Proceeding as in the proof of [
15
, Page 14, Proposition 3.1], for some constants
\displaystyle c=c(p)>0
and
\displaystyle C=C(p)>0
, we have

{}I=\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(w\psi^{p})dx\geq c\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}|\nabla w|^{p}dx-C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}w^{p}|\nabla\psi|^{p}dx.

(3.3)

Moreover, from the lines of the proof of [
24
, Pages 24-25, Lemma 3.1], for some constants
\displaystyle c=c(p,q,s,n,\Lambda)>0
and
\displaystyle C=C(p,q,s,n,\Lambda)>0
, we have

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}J&=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(w(x)\psi(x)^{p}-w(y)\psi(y)^{p})d\mu\\
&\geq&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|w(x)-w(y)|^{q-2}(w(x)-w(y))(w(x)\psi(x)^{p}-w(y)\psi(y)^{p})d\mu\\
&\geq&c\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|w(x)\psi^{p/q}(x)-w(y)\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\\
&&\quad-C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\max\{w(x),w(y)\}^{q}|\psi^{p/q}(x)-\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\\
&&\quad-Cr^{-sq}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}w^{q}dx-C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{w(y)^{q-1}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy\right)w\psi^{p}dx.\end{array}

(3.4)

By applying
Eqs.

3.3
and
3.4
in
Eq.

3.2
, we obtain
Eq.

3.1
. In the case of a weak supersolution, the estimate in
Eq.

3.1
follows by applying the obtained result to
\displaystyle-u

∎

Remark 3.2
.

It should be noted that the same form of energy estimate also holds for the general operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
described in
Eq.

2.5
. The proof follows precisely similar to [
24
, Lemma 3.1].

Next, we show the local boundedness of weak solutions. We apply the following real analysis lemma. For the proof, see [
27
, Lemma 4.1].

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\displaystyle\left(Y_{i}\right)_{i=0}^{\infty}
be a sequence of positive real numbers such that
\displaystyle Y_{0}\leq

\displaystyle c_{0}^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}b^{-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}}
and
\displaystyle Y_{i+1}\leq c_{0}b^{i}Y_{i}^{1+\beta},i=0,1,2,\ldots
, for some constants
\displaystyle c_{0},b>1
and
\displaystyle\beta>0
. Then
\displaystyle\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}Y_{i}=0
.

Theorem 3.4
.

(Local boundedness). Let
\displaystyle 1<q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle u
be a local weak subsolution of
Eq.

2.3
. Then there exists a positive constant
\displaystyle c=c(n,p,q,s)
, such that

{}\underset{B_{\frac{r}{2}}(x_{0})}{\operatorname{ess}\sup}\,u\leq\operatorname{Tail}_{q-1}(u^{+};x_{0},\frac{r}{2})^{\frac{q-1}{p-1}}+c\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}(u^{+})^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}^{\frac{1}{p-1}}+1,

(3.5)

whenever
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\subset\Omega
with
\displaystyle r\in(0,1]
. Moreover, if
\displaystyle u
is a weak supersolution, then
Eq.

3.5
holds with
\displaystyle-u
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
with
\displaystyle r\in(0,1]
. For
\displaystyle i=0,1,2,\ldots
, let us denote
\displaystyle r_{i}=\frac{r}{2}(1+

\displaystyle\left.2^{-i}\right),\bar{r}_{i}=\frac{r_{i}+r_{i+1}}{2},B_{i}=B_{r_{i}}(x_{0})
and
\displaystyle\bar{B}_{i}=B_{\bar{r}_{i}}(x_{0})
. Let
\displaystyle(\psi_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\bar{B}_{i})
be a sequence of cutoff functions such that
\displaystyle 0\leq\psi_{i}\leq 1
in
\displaystyle\bar{B}_{i},\psi_{i}=1
in
\displaystyle B_{i+1}
and
\displaystyle|\nabla\psi_{i}|\leq\frac{2^{i+3}}{r}
for every
\displaystyle i=0,1,2,\ldots
. For
\displaystyle i=0,1,2,\ldots
and
\displaystyle k,\bar{k}\geq 0
, we denote
\displaystyle k_{i}=k+(1-2^{-i})\bar{k},\bar{k}_{i}=\frac{k_{i}+k_{i+1}}{2},w_{i}=\left(u-k_{i}\right)^{+}
and
\displaystyle\bar{w}_{i}=\left(u-\bar{k}_{i}\right)^{+}
. Then
\displaystyle\exists\,c=c(n,p)>0
such that

{}\left(\frac{\bar{k}}{2^{i+2}}\right)^{\frac{p(\kappa-1)}{\kappa}}\left(\fint_{B_{i+1}}w_{i+1}^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}}=\left(k_{i+1}-\bar{k}_{i}\right)^{\frac{p(\kappa-1)}{\kappa}}\left(\fint_{B_{i+1}}w_{i+1}^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}}\leq c\left(\fint_{\bar{B}_{i}}|\bar{w}_{i}\psi_{i}|^{p\kappa}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}},

(3.6)

where
\displaystyle\kappa
is given by
Eq.

2.2
. Next by Sobolev inequality in
Theorem

2.11
, with
\displaystyle c=c(n,p,s)>0
, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\left(\fint_{\bar{B}_{i}}|\bar{w}_{i}\psi_{i}|^{p\kappa}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}}\leq cr^{p-n}\int_{B_{i}}|\nabla(\bar{w}_{i}\psi_{i})|^{p}dx\leq cr^{p-n}\left(\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}^{p}|\nabla\psi_{i}|^{p}dx+\int_{B_{i}}\psi_{i}^{p}|\nabla\bar{w}_{i}|^{p}dx\right)=I_{1}+I_{2}.\end{array}

(3.7)

Estimate of
\displaystyle I_{1}
:
Using the properties of
\displaystyle\psi_{i}
, for some
\displaystyle c=c(n,p,s)>0
, we have

{}I_{1}=cr^{p-n}\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}^{p}|\nabla\psi_{i}|^{p}dx\leq c2^{ip}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}dx.

(3.8)

Estimate of
\displaystyle I_{2}
:
By
Eq.

3.1
, with
\displaystyle c=c(n,p,s)
and
\displaystyle C=(n,p,q,s)
positive, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}I_{2}&=&cr^{p-n}\int_{B_{i}}\psi_{i}^{p}|\nabla\bar{w}_{i}|^{p}dx\\
&\leq&Cr^{p-n}\left(\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}^{p}|\nabla\psi_{i}|^{p}dx+\int_{B_{i}}\int_{B_{i}}\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(x),\bar{w}_{i}(y)\}^{q}|\psi_{i}^{p/q}(x)-\psi_{i}^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\right.\\
&&\left.+\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}(x)\psi_{i}(x)^{p}dx\cdot\underset{x\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}}{\operatorname{ess}sup}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{i}}\frac{\bar{w}_{i}(y)^{q-1}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy+r^{-sq}\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}^{q}dx+\int_{B_{i}}|f|\bar{w}_{i}\psi_{i}^{p}dx\right)\\
&=&J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{5}.\end{array}

(3.9)

Estimates of
\displaystyle J_{1},J_{2}
and
\displaystyle J_{4}
:
To estimate
\displaystyle J_{1}
, we use the estimate of
\displaystyle I_{1}
in
Eq.

3.8
above and to estimate
\displaystyle J_{2}
, noticing the bounds on
\displaystyle\psi_{i}
and the relation

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{i}^{q}\leq\frac{w_{i}^{p}}{(\bar{k}_{i}-k_{i})^{p-q}},

we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\int_{B_{i}}\int_{B_{i}}\frac{|\psi_{i}^{p/q}(x)-\psi_{i}^{p/q}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(x),\bar{w}_{i}(y)\}^{q}dxdy&\leq&2c\frac{2^{iq}}{r^{q}}\int_{B_{i}}\frac{w_{i}^{p}(x)}{(\bar{k}_{i}-k_{i})^{p-q}}\left(\int_{B_{i}}\frac{dy}{|x-y|^{n+sq-q}}\right)dx\\
&\leq&c\frac{2^{i(q+p-q)}}{q(1-s)}\frac{1}{\bar{k}^{p-q}}\frac{r^{n}}{r^{sq}}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}(x)dx.\end{array}

Finally for
\displaystyle J_{4}
, we estimate as

J_{4}=Cr^{p-n-sq}\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}^{q}dx\leq Cr^{p-n-sq}\int_{B_{i}}\frac{w_{i}^{p}(x)}{(\bar{k}_{i}-k_{i})^{p-q}}dx\leq Cr^{p-sq}\frac{2^{i(p-q)}}{\bar{k}^{p-q}}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}(x)dx,

so that we have

{}J_{j}\leq c(n,p,q,s)2^{ip}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}dx,\quad j=1,2,4.

(3.10)

Estimate of
\displaystyle J_{3}
:
For
\displaystyle x\in\bar{B}_{i}=\operatorname{supp}(\psi_{i})
and
\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|y-x_{0}|}{|x-y|}\leq 1+\frac{|x-x_{0}|}{|x-y|}\leq 1+\frac{\bar{r}_{i}}{r_{i}-\bar{r}_{i}}\leq 2^{i+4}.

Then, using the relation

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{i}\leq\frac{w_{i}^{p}}{(\bar{k}_{i}-k_{i})^{p-1}},

we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{r}\int_{B_{i}}\bar{w}_{i}(x)\psi_{i}(x)^{p}dx\cdot\underset{x\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{i}}{\operatorname{ess}sup}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{i}}\frac{\bar{w}_{i}(y)^{q-1}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy\leq c\frac{2^{i(n+sq)}}{(\bar{k}_{i}-k_{i})^{p-1}}\left(\int_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}(x)dx\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{i}}\frac{\bar{w}_{i}(y)^{q-1}dy}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}\right)\\
\quad\leq c(n,p,q,s)\frac{2^{i(n+sq+p-1)}}{\bar{k}^{p-1}}\frac{r^{n}}{r^{sq}}T_{q-1}(w_{0};x_{0},\frac{r}{2})^{q-1}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}(x)dx.\end{array}

(3.11)

Estimate of
\displaystyle J_{5}
:
Proceeding similarly to above, we obtain

{}\int_{B_{i}}|f(x)|\bar{w}_{i}(x)\psi_{i}^{p}(x)dx\leq c(n,p,q,s)\frac{2^{i(p-1)}}{\bar{k}^{p-1}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}r^{n}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}(x)dx.

(3.12)

Substituting the estimate of
Eqs.

3.10
,
3.11
and
3.12
into
Eq.

3.9
, we get

{}I_{2}\leq c(n,p,q,s)2^{i(n+sq+p-1)}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}dx,

(3.13)

whenever

\displaystyle\bar{k}\geq T_{q-1}(w_{0};x_{0},\frac{r}{2})^{\frac{q-1}{p-1}}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}^{1/(p-1)}+1.

Inserting
Eqs.

3.8
and
3.13
into
Eq.

3.7
we have

{}\left(\fint_{\bar{B}_{i}}|\bar{w}_{i}\psi_{i}|^{p\kappa}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}}\leq c(n,p,q,s)2^{i(n+sq+p-1)}\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}dx.

(3.14)

Setting

Y_{i}=\left(\fint_{B_{i}}w_{i}^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bar{k}=T_{q-1}(w_{0};x_{0},\frac{r}{2})^{\frac{q-1}{p-1}}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}^{1/(p-1)}+1+c_{0}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}b^{\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}w_{0}^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where

c_{0}=c(n,p,q,s),\quad b=2^{\left(\frac{n+sq+p-1}{p}+\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\right)\kappa}\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta=\kappa-1,

from
Eqs.

3.6
and
3.14
we obtain

\frac{Y_{i+1}}{\bar{k}}\leq c(n,p,q,s)2^{i\left(\frac{n+sq+p-1}{p}+\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\right)\kappa}\left(\frac{Y_{i}}{\bar{k}}\right)^{\kappa}.

Moreover, by the definition of
\displaystyle\bar{k}
above we have

\frac{Y_{0}}{\bar{k}}\leq c_{0}^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}b^{-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}}.

Thus from
Lemma

3.3
, we obtain
\displaystyle Y_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
\displaystyle i\rightarrow\infty
. This implies that

\underset{B_{\frac{r}{2}}(x_{0})}{\operatorname{ess}\sup}\,u\leq k+\bar{k},

which gives
Eq.

3.5
by choosing
\displaystyle k=0
.
Analogous treatment for
\displaystyle(u-k)^{-}
gives the lower bound of
\displaystyle u
in
\displaystyle B_{r/2}(x_{0})
. This implies
\displaystyle u\in L^{\infty}(B_{r/2}(x_{0}))
. A standard covering argument gives
\displaystyle u\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
.
∎

Now we prove
Theorem

2.17
. We first fix some notations which will be used in this subsection. For a measurable function
\displaystyle u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define

u_{h}(x)=u(x+h),\quad\delta_{h}u(x)=u_{h}(x)-u(x),\quad\delta_{h}^{2}u(x)=\delta_{h}(\delta_{h}u(x))=u_{2h}(x)+u(x)-2u_{h}(x).

Now, we recall the discrete Leibniz rule

\delta_{h}(uv)=u_{h}\delta_{h}v+v\delta_{h}u\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta_{h}^{2}(uv)=u_{2h}\delta_{h}^{2}v+2\delta_{h}v\delta_{h}u_{h}+v\delta_{h}^{2}u.

The following lemma iteration lemma will be useful.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle 0<s<1
. Suppose that
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}\left(B_{2}(x_{0})\right)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a local weak solution of
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u=f
in
\displaystyle B_{2}(x_{0})
. Let
\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_{2}(x_{0}))
and

{}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(x_{0}))}\leq 1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(y)|^{q-1}}{|y|^{n+sq}}dy\leq 1,\quad\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(x_{0}))}\leq 1.

(3.15)

Let
\displaystyle 0<h_{0}<{1}/{10}
and
\displaystyle R
be such that
\displaystyle 4h_{0}<R\leq 1-5h_{0}
and
\displaystyle\nabla u\in L^{m}(B_{R+4h_{0}}(x_{0}))
for some
\displaystyle m\geq p
. Then

\sup_{0<|h|<h_{0}}\left\|\frac{\delta_{h}^{2}u}{|h|^{1+\frac{1}{m+1}}}\right\|_{L^{m+1}(B_{R-4h_{0}}(x_{0}))}^{m+1}\leq C\left(\int_{B_{R+4h_{0}(x_{0})}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+1\right),

for some constant
\displaystyle C=Cn,h_{0},p,q,m,s)>0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, one can assume
\displaystyle x_{0}=0
. Let
\displaystyle r=R-4h_{0}
and
\displaystyle\phi\in W^{1,p}(B_{R})
vanish outside
\displaystyle B_{\frac{R+r}{2}}
. Since
\displaystyle u
is a local weak solution of
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u=f
in
\displaystyle B_{2}
, we have

{}\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}dxdy=\int_{B_{R}}f\phi\,dx.

(3.16)

Let
\displaystyle h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
be such that
\displaystyle|h|<h_{0}
. Choosing
\displaystyle\phi_{-h}
as a test function and using a change of variable, we have

{}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\nabla u_{h}\right|^{p-2}\nabla u_{h}\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y)|^{q-2}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}dxdy=\int_{B_{R}}f_{h}\phi\,dx.

(3.17)

Subtracting
Eq.

3.16
with
Eq.

3.17
and dividing the resulting equation by
\displaystyle|h|
, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{(|\nabla u_{h}|^{p-2}\nabla u_{h}-|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u)}{|h|}\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{(J_{q}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))-J_{q}(u(x)-u(y)))}{|h|}(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu\\
=\int_{B_{R}}(f(x+h)-f(x))\phi\,dx,\end{array}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\forall\phi\in W^{1,p}(B_{R})
vanishing outside
\displaystyle B_{\frac{R+r}{2}}
. We have denoted
\displaystyle J_{\beta}(t)=|t|^{\beta-2}t,\beta>1,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle d\mu=\frac{dxdy}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}
. Let
\displaystyle\eta
be a nonnegative Lipschitz cut-off function such that

\displaystyle\eta\equiv 1\text{ on }B_{r},\eta\equiv 0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{\frac{R+r}{2}},|\nabla\eta|\leq\frac{C}{R-r}=\frac{C}{4h_{0}},

for some constant
\displaystyle C=C(n)>0
. Suppose
\displaystyle\alpha\geq 1,\theta>0
and testing
Eq.

3.18
with

\displaystyle\phi=J_{\alpha+1}\left(\frac{u_{h}-u}{|h|^{\theta}}\right)\eta^{p},0<|h|<h_{0},

we get

{}I+J=F

(3.19)

where

\begin{array}[]{l}I=\int_{B_{R}}\frac{(|\nabla u_{h}|^{p-2}\nabla u_{h}-|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u)}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}\cdot\nabla(J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}-u)\eta^{p})dx,\\
J=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{(J_{q}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))-J_{q}(u(x)-u(y)))}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}\times(J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(x)-u(x))\eta^{p}(x)-J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(y)-u(y))\eta^{p}(y))d\mu\end{array}

and

F=\int_{B_{R}}\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}J_{\alpha+1}({u_{h}-u})\eta^{p}\,dx.

Proceeding similarly like [
33
, Proposition 4.1, Step 2] one gets

{}I\geq c\int_{B_{R}}\left|\nabla\left(\frac{|u_{h}-u|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{p}}(u_{h}-u)\eta}{|h|^{\frac{1+\theta\alpha}{p}}}\right)\right|^{p}dx-C\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u_{h}|^{m}dx-C\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx-C\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-p+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-p+2}}}dx-C

(3.20)

for
\displaystyle c=c(p,\alpha)>0
and
\displaystyle C=C(n,h_{0},p,m,\alpha)>0
.

Estimate of the nonlocal integral
\displaystyle J
:
First, we notice that

\displaystyle J=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3},

where

\begin{array}[]{l}J_{1}=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{(J_{q}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))-J_{q}(u(x)-u(y)))}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}(J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(x)-u(x))\eta^{p}(x)-J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(y)-u(y))\eta^{p}(y))d\mu,\\
J_{2}=\int_{B_{\frac{R+r}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{R}}\frac{(J_{q}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))-J_{q}(u(x)-u(y)))}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(x)-u(x))\eta^{p}(x)d\mu\end{array}

and

J_{3}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{R}}\int_{B_{\frac{R+r}{2}}}\frac{(J_{q}(u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y))-J_{q}(u(x)-u(y)))}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}J_{\alpha+1}(u_{h}(y)-u(y))\eta^{p}(y)d\mu.

Proceeding similarly like [
35
, Proposition 3.9, Step 1], we get

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}J_{1}&\geq&c\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\frac{J_{\frac{\alpha+q-1}{q}}(\delta_{h}u(x))}{|h|^{\frac{1+\theta\alpha}{q}}}-\frac{J_{\frac{\alpha+q-1}{q}}(\delta_{h}u(y))}{|h|^{\frac{1+\theta\alpha}{q}}}\right|^{q}(\eta^{p}(x)+\eta^{p}(y))d\mu\\
&&-C\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha+1}+|\delta_{h}u(y)|^{\alpha+1}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}\left(|u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y)|^{\frac{q-2}{2}}+|u(x)-u(y)|^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\right)^{2}|\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(y)|^{2}d\mu.\end{array}

(3.21)

Noting that the first term on the right hand side is non-negative, we estimate the second term only. Using the bounds on
\displaystyle\eta
and
\displaystyle|\nabla\eta|
, we deduce that

J_{11}=\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha+1}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}|\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(y)|^{2}d\mu\leq\frac{C}{h_{0}^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}}{|x-y|^{n+sq-2}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha+1}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}dxdy.

For
\displaystyle q=2
, noticing the fact that
\displaystyle R<1
, we have

{}J_{11}\leq\frac{C(n,s)}{h_{0}^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+h_{0}})}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}dx.

(3.22)

For
\displaystyle q>2
, take

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon<\min\left\{q/2-1,1/s-1\right\}
and use Young’s inequality (with exponents
\displaystyle m/(q-2)
and
\displaystyle m/(m-q+2)
), to have

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}J_{11}&\leq&\frac{C}{h_{0}^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}}{|x-y|^{n+sq-2}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha+1}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}dxdy\\
&\leq&\frac{C}{h_{0}^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{m}}{|x-y|^{n+ms\frac{q-2-\epsilon}{q-2}}}dxdy+\frac{C}{h_{0}^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}|x-y|^{-n+\frac{m(2-2s-\epsilon s)}{m-q+2}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\frac{(\alpha+1)m}{m-q+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-q+2}}}dxdy\\
&\leq&C[u]^{m}_{W^{\frac{s(q-2-\epsilon)}{q-2},m}(B_{R+h_{0}})}+C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+h_{0})}}^{\frac{m}{m-q+2}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-q+2}}}dx,\end{array}

(3.23)

where
\displaystyle C=C\left(n,s,q,h_{0}\right)>0
is a constant (which depends inversely on
\displaystyle h_{0}
), and in the last line, we have used the fact that

\displaystyle\frac{m(2-2s-\epsilon s)}{m-q+2}>0.

For the first term, we use Sobolev embedding of
Lemma

2.3
to have

{}[u]^{m}_{W^{\frac{s(q-2-\epsilon)}{q-2},m}(B_{R+h_{0}})}\leq C\int_{B_{R+h_{0}}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+h_{0}})}^{m}.

(3.24)

A similar estimate gives

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}J_{12}&=&\int_{B_{R}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\alpha+1}}{|h|^{1+\theta\alpha}}|u_{h}(x)-u_{h}(y)|^{q-2}|\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(x)-\eta^{\frac{p}{2}}(y)|^{2}d\mu\\
&\leq&C\int_{B_{R+4h_{0}}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+4h_{0}})}^{m}+C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R+4h_{0})}}^{\frac{m}{m-q+2}}\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-q+2}}}dx.\end{array}

(3.25)

Combining
Eqs.

3.22
,
3.23
,
3.24
and
3.25
and putting in
Eq.

3.21
together with Young’s inequality, we obtain using
Eq.

3.15

{}J_{1}\geq-C\left[\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-q+2}}}dx+\int_{B_{R+4h_{0}}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+1\right]

(3.26)

where
\displaystyle C=C(n,q,s,h_{0})>0
is a constant (which depends inversely on
\displaystyle h_{0}
). Proceed now as [
35
, Proposition 3.9, Step 3], to get

{}|J_{2}|+|J_{3}|+|F|\leq C\left(\|u\|^{q-1}_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}}\frac{|u(y)|^{q-1}}{|y|^{n+sq}}dy+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\right)\left[1+\int_{B_{R}}\frac{|\delta_{h}u(x)|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)m}{m-q+2}}}dx\right]

(3.27)

Putting
Eqs.

3.20
,
3.26
and
3.27
in
Eq.

3.19
, we get

\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\nabla\left(\frac{|u_{h}-u|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{p}}(u_{h}-u)\eta}{|h|^{\frac{1+\theta\alpha}{p}}}\right)\right|^{p}dx\\
\leq C\left(\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u_{h}|^{m}dx+\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+\int_{B_{R}}\left|\frac{\delta_{h}u}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)}{\alpha}}}\right|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-p+2}}dx+1\right)\\
\quad+C\left(\int_{B_{R}}\left|\frac{\delta_{h}u}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)}{\alpha}}}\right|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}dx+\int_{B_{R+4h_{0}}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+1\right)+C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\left(1+\int_{B_{R}}\left|\frac{\delta_{h}u}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)}{\alpha}}}\right|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-q+2}}dx\right)\\
\leq C\left(\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u_{h}|^{m}dx+\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+\int_{B_{R+4h_{0}}}|\nabla u|^{m}dx+\int_{B_{R}}\left|\frac{\delta_{h}u}{|h|^{\frac{(1+\theta\alpha)}{\alpha}}}\right|^{\frac{\alpha m}{m-p+2}}dx+1\right),\end{array}

for some
\displaystyle C=C(n,h_{0},p,q,m,s,\alpha)
. We have used Young’s inequality with exponents
\displaystyle\frac{m-q+2}{m-p+2}
and
\displaystyle\frac{m-q+2}{p-q}
in the last line. One can now proceed as [
33
, Proposition 4.1, Step 4 and 5] to get the desired result.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

(Estimate of local seminorm)
Let
\displaystyle 2\leq q\leq p<\infty
and
\displaystyle 0<s<1
. Suppose that
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(B_{2}(x_{0}))\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a local weak solution of
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u=f
in
\displaystyle B_{2}(x_{0})
. Let
\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_{2}(x_{0}))
and

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(x_{0}))}\leq 1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(y)|^{q-1}}{|y|^{n+sq}}dy\leq 1,\quad\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(x_{0}))}\leq 1.

Then

\int_{B_{\frac{7}{8}}\left(x_{0}\right)}|\nabla u|^{p}dx\leq C(n,p,q,s).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\displaystyle x_{0}=0
. We only provide the proof for
\displaystyle w=u^{+}
, the proof of
\displaystyle u^{-}
is similar. We apply
Lemma

3.1
with
\displaystyle r=1,x_{0}=0,k=0
and with
\displaystyle\psi\in

\displaystyle C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{8}{9}})
such that
\displaystyle\psi=1
on
\displaystyle B_{\frac{7}{8}},0\leq\psi\leq 1
and
\displaystyle|\nabla\psi|\leq C
for some
\displaystyle C=C(n)>0
. By using the properties of
\displaystyle\psi
, this yields

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\int_{B_{\frac{7}{8}}}|\nabla u|^{p}dx&\leq&C(n,p,q,s)\left(\int_{B_{1}}|u|^{p}dx+\int_{B_{1}}|u|^{q}dx+\int_{B_{1}}\int_{B_{1}}\left(|u(x)|^{p}+|u(y)|^{q}\right)|x-y|^{q-sq-n}dxdy\right)\\
&&+C(n,p,q,s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}}\frac{|u(y)|^{q-1}}{|y|^{n+sq}}dy\int_{B_{\frac{8}{9}}}|u(x)|dx+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\int_{B_{1}}w\,dx\\
&\leq&C(n,p,q,s)(1+C(q,s)+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}).\end{array}

Hence, the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Lemma

3.5
and
Lemma

3.6
holds for the operator
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}+A(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}
for
\displaystyle 0<A\leq 1
which can be proved following the same lines.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

2.17
.

Proof of
Theorem

2.17

We first observe that
\displaystyle u\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, by
Theorem

3.4
. We assume for simplicity that
\displaystyle x_{0}=0
, then we set
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}}=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{0}})}+\operatorname{Tail}_{q-1}(u;0,R_{0})+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R_{0}})}+1>0.

It is sufficient to consider the rescaled functions

u_{R_{0}}(x):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}}}u(R_{0}x),\quad f_{R_{0}}(x):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}}}f(R_{0}x),\quad\text{ for }x\in B_{2},

and to show that
\displaystyle u_{R_{0}}
satisfies the estimate

\left[u_{R_{0}}\right]_{C^{\sigma}(B_{1/2})}\leq C.

By scaling back, we would get the desired estimate. Note that by definition, the function
\displaystyle u_{R_{0}}
is a local weak solution of
\displaystyle-\Delta_{p}u_{R_{0}}+R_{0}^{p-qs}\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}}^{q-p}(-\Delta_{q})^{s}u_{R_{0}}=R_{0}^{p}\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}}^{2-p}f_{R_{0}}=\tilde{f}
in
\displaystyle B_{2}
and satisfies (by
Lemma

3.6
; note that as
\displaystyle 2\leq p
, so
\displaystyle\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\leq 1
)

\|u_{R_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\leq 1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}}\frac{|u_{R_{0}}(y)|^{q-1}}{|y|^{n+sq}}dy\leq 1,\quad[u_{R_{0}}]_{W^{1,p}(B_{\frac{7}{8}})}\leq C(n,p,q,s).

By noting
Remark

3.7
, the rest of the proof follows similarly like [
33
, Theorem 1.3].

4
Improved Hölder regularity results

We now relax the condition on the source term
\displaystyle f
to be in
\displaystyle L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and take
\displaystyle f\in L^{n}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. For a function
\displaystyle g\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and any
\displaystyle B_{r}(x_{0})\subset\subset\Omega
, define

\begin{array}[]{c}(g)_{B_{r}(x_{0})}=\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}g\,dx,\quad\operatorname{av}_{m}(g,B_{r}(x_{0})):=\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|g-(g)_{B_{r}}|^{m}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{m}},m>0\\
\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(r)\equiv\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(g,B_{r}(x_{0})):=r^{\frac{\delta}{q}}\left(r^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|g(y)-(g)_{B_{r}}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}},\delta\geq sq.\end{array}

To prove Hölder regularity results for
Eq.

2.5
, we adopt the perturbation techniques used in
[
24
]
. The set-up used in
[
24
]
needs the solution to be in
\displaystyle W^{s,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, while we will prove the results for local solutions only. The following important lemma will be the most crucial for our case.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\displaystyle B_{t}(x_{0})\subset B_{r}(x_{0})
be two concentric balls,
\displaystyle q\geq 2,\delta\geq sq
and
\displaystyle g\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{sq}^{q-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(a) There exists
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,q)>0
such that, whenever
\displaystyle 0<t<r\leq 1
, it holds

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(g,B_{t}(x_{0}))\leq c\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\delta/q}\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(g,B_{r}(x_{0}))+ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\int_{t}^{r}\left(\frac{t}{v}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(g,B_{v}(x_{0}))\frac{dv}{v}+\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(g,B_{r}(x_{0}))\right).\end{array}

(4.1)

(b) With
\displaystyle m\geq 1
, if
\displaystyle v>0
and
\displaystyle\theta\in(0,1)
are such that
\displaystyle\theta r\leq v\leq r
, then

{}\operatorname{av}_{{m}}(g,B_{v}(x_{0}))\leq 2\theta^{-n/{m}}\operatorname{av}_{{m}}(g,B_{r}(x_{0})).

(4.2)

Proof.

In what follows, all the balls will be centred at
\displaystyle x_{0}
. Let us first recall the standard property

{}\left(\fint_{B_{r}}|g-(g)_{B_{r}}|^{m}dx\right)^{1/m}\leq 2\left(\fint_{B_{r}}|g-k|^{m}dx\right)^{1/m}

(4.3)

that holds
\displaystyle\forall k\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle m\geq 1
; from this
Eq.

4.2
follows immediately. To prove
Eq.

4.1
, let
\displaystyle B_{t}\subset B_{r}
, we then use

\displaystyle d\lambda_{x_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{t})=c(n,s,q)t^{-sq},d\lambda_{x_{0}}(x):=\frac{dx}{|x-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}

and split

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(t)&\leq&c\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\delta/q}r^{\delta/q}\left(t^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{r}}\frac{|g(y)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}+ct^{\delta/q}\left(t^{s}\int_{B_{r}\backslash B_{t}}\frac{|g(y)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\\
&\leq&c\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\delta/q}\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(r)+ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}|(g)_{B_{t}}-(g)_{B_{r}}|+ct^{\delta/q}\left(t^{s}\int_{B_{r}\backslash B_{t}}\frac{|g(y)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+sq}}dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\\
&=&c\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\delta/q}\operatorname{snail}_{\delta}(r)+cT_{1}+cT_{2},\end{array}

where
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,q)
. If
\displaystyle r/4\leq t<r
, standard manipulations based on
Eq.

4.2
gives
\displaystyle T_{1}+T_{2}\leq ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(g,B_{r})
with
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,q)
. We can therefore assume that
\displaystyle t<r/4
. Then there exists

\displaystyle\lambda\in(1/4,1/2)
and
\displaystyle\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
\displaystyle\kappa\geq 2
so that
\displaystyle t=\lambda^{\kappa}r
. We now use triangle and Hölder’s
inequalities, and estimate, using
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.3
repeatedly

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}T_{1}&\leq&t^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(|(g)_{B_{\lambda r}}-(g)_{B_{r}}|+|(g)_{B_{\lambda r}}-(g)_{B_{\lambda^{\kappa}r}}|\right)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)+\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}|(g)_{B_{\lambda^{i}r}}-(g)_{B_{\lambda^{i+1}r}}|\right)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)+\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}\left(\fint_{B_{\lambda^{i}r}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{\lambda^{i}r}}|^{q}dx\right)^{1/q}\right)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)+\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}\int_{\lambda^{i}r}^{\lambda^{i-1}r}\operatorname{av}_{q}(\lambda^{i}r)\frac{dv}{v}\right)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)+\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa-1}\int_{\lambda^{i}r}^{\lambda^{i-1}r}\operatorname{av}_{q}(v)\frac{dv}{v}\right)\\
&\leq&\leq ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\left(\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)+\int_{t}^{r}\operatorname{av}_{q}(v)\frac{dv}{v}\right),\end{array}

(4.4)

with
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,q)
. For
\displaystyle T_{2}
, we rewrite
\displaystyle r=\lambda^{-\kappa}t
and estimate, by telescoping and Jensen’s inequality

{}\left(\fint_{B_{\lambda^{-i}t}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q}dx\right)^{1/q}\leq 2^{n/q+1}\sum_{m=0}^{i}\operatorname{av}_{q}(\lambda^{-m}t),

(4.5)

for
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq k
. Then, using
Eq.

4.3
,
Eq.

4.5
and the discrete Fubini theorem, we get by noting
\displaystyle 0<\lambda<1
and
\displaystyle q-1\geq 1
,

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}T_{2}&\leq&ct^{\delta/q}\left(t^{s}\sum_{i=0}^{\kappa-1}\lambda^{isq}(\lambda^{-i}t)^{-n}t^{-sq}\int_{B_{\lambda^{-i-1}t}\backslash B_{\lambda^{-i}t}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\kappa-1}\lambda^{isq}\fint_{B_{\lambda^{-i-1}t}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{i=0}^{\kappa-1}\left(\lambda^{isq}\fint_{B_{\lambda^{-i-1}t}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q-1}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q-1}}\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{i=0}^{\kappa-1}\lambda^{\frac{isq}{q-1}}\left(\fint_{B_{\lambda^{-i-1}t}}|g(x)-(g)_{B_{t}}|^{q}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{i=0}^{\kappa}\lambda^{is}\sum_{m=0}^{i}\operatorname{av}_{q}(\lambda^{-m}t)\\
&=&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{m=0}^{\kappa}\operatorname{av}_{q}(\lambda^{-m}t)\sum_{i=m}^{\kappa}\lambda^{is}\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{m=0}^{\kappa}\lambda^{ms}\operatorname{av}_{q}(\lambda^{-m}t)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{m=0}^{\kappa-1}\int_{\lambda^{-m}t}^{\lambda^{-m-1}t}\lambda^{ms}\operatorname{av}_{q}(v)\frac{dv}{v}+ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\sum_{m=0}^{\kappa-1}\int_{\lambda^{-m}t}^{\lambda^{-m-1}t}\left(\frac{t}{v}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(v)\frac{dv}{v}+ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(r)\\
&\leq&ct^{\delta/q-s}\int_{t}^{r}\left(\frac{t}{v}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(v)\frac{dv}{v}+ct^{\delta/q-s}\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{s}\operatorname{av}_{q}(r),\end{array}

(4.6)

for
\displaystyle c\equiv c(n,s,q)
. Merging the
Eqs.

4.4
and
4.6
we obtain
Eq.

4.1
.
∎

From hereafter, we take
\displaystyle u
as a local weak solution to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}u=f
in
\displaystyle\Omega
. This assumption holds until the end of Hölder regularity results. Before Proceeding further, we note
Remark

3.2
and take
\displaystyle k=(u)_{B_{r}}
in
Lemma

3.1
and set
\displaystyle\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{r})
such that
\displaystyle 0\leq\psi\leq 1
in
\displaystyle B_{r}
,
\displaystyle\psi\equiv 1
in
\displaystyle B_{r/2}
,
\displaystyle\psi\equiv 0
outside
\displaystyle B_{3r/4}
and
\displaystyle|\nabla\psi|\leq{C}/{r}
for some constant
\displaystyle C>0
. Then
Eq.

3.1
gives

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\fint_{B_{r}}\psi^{p}|\nabla u|^{p}dx+\fint_{B_{r/2}}\int_{B_{r/2}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}dxdy\\
\leq C\left(r^{-p}\fint_{B_{r}}|u-(u)_{B_{r}}|^{p}dx+\fint_{B_{r}}\int_{B_{r}}\max\{|u(x)-(u)_{B_{r}}|,|u(y)-(u)_{B_{r}}|\}^{q}|\psi^{p/q}(x)-\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\right.\\
\left.\quad+\fint_{B_{r}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{r}}\frac{|u(y)-(u)_{B_{r}}|^{q-1}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dy\right)|u(x)-(u)_{B_{r}}|\psi^{p}dx\right.\\
\left.\quad+r^{-sq}\fint_{B_{r}}|u-(u)_{B_{r}}|^{q}dx+\fint_{B_{r}}|f||u-(u)_{B_{r}}|\psi^{p}dx\right)\\
=I+II+III+IV+V,\end{array}

(4.7)

for some
\displaystyle C\equiv C(n,p,q,s,\Lambda)
.

Estimate of
\displaystyle II
:
Noting the properties of
\displaystyle\psi
, one gets

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\quad\fint_{B_{r}}\int_{B_{r}}\max\{|u(x)-(u)_{B_{r}}|,|u(y)-(u)_{B_{r}}|\}^{q}|\psi^{p/q}(x)-\psi^{p/q}(y)|^{q}d\mu\\
\leq Cr^{-q}\fint_{B_{r}}\int_{B_{r}}\frac{\max\{|u(x)-(u)_{B_{r}}|,|u(y)-(u)_{B_{r}}|\}^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq-q}}dxdy\leq Cr^{-sq}\fint_{B_{r}}|u-(u)_{B_{r}}|^{q}dx,\end{array}

(4.8)

for some
\displaystyle C\equiv C(n,p,q,s)
.

Estimate of
\displaystyle III
:
Note that
