{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}I&=&\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\psi_{\epsilon}\,dx\\
&=&\int_{\Omega}\phi_{K}(A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})-A(x,\nabla u))\cdot\nabla T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))dx+\int_{\Omega}\phi_{K}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))dx\\
&&+\int_{\Omega}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi_{K}dx=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.\end{array}

(7.11)

Estimate of
\displaystyle I_{2}
:
As
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\rightharpoonup u
weakly in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
we get
\displaystyle T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))\rightharpoonup 0
weakly in
\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. As a consequence using
Eq.

2.6
, we get

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}I_{2}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{K}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))dx=0.

(7.12)

Estimate of
\displaystyle I_{3}
:
For
\displaystyle\omega=\operatorname{supp}\phi_{K}
, by Hölder’s inequality and
Eq.

2.6
, we use the uniform boundedness of
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\omega)
, to have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left|I_{3}\right|&=&\left|\int_{\Omega}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi_{K}dx\right|\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi_{K}||u_{\epsilon}-u||\nabla u_{\epsilon}|^{p-1}dx\\
&\leq&\|\nabla\phi_{K}\|_{L^{\infty}(\omega)}\left(\int_{\omega}|\nabla u_{\epsilon}|^{p}dx\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\|u_{\epsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\omega)}\leq C\|u_{\epsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(\omega)},\end{array}

for some constant
\displaystyle C>0
, independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
. As
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\to u
strongly in
\displaystyle L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
so

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}I_{3}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi_{K}dx=0.

(7.13)

Therefore, merging
Eq.

7.11
,
Eq.

7.12
and
Eq.

7.13
we obtain

{}\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}I=\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}I_{1}=\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{K}(A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})-A(x,\nabla u))\cdot\nabla T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))dx.

(7.14)

Estimate of
\displaystyle J
:

Denoting by
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}g(x,y)=|g(x)-g(y)|^{q-2}(g(x)-g(y))
and
\displaystyle d\nu=\frac{dxdy}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}
, we can write
\displaystyle J
as:

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}J&=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\psi_{\epsilon}(x)-\psi_{\epsilon}(y))d\nu\\
&=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{K}(x)(\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)-\mathcal{A}u(x,y))(T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(x))-T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y)))d\nu\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi_{K}(y)-\phi_{K}(x))d\nu\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(x))-\phi_{K}(y)T_{\nu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y)))d\nu:=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}.\end{array}

(7.15)

Using the monotonicity of the map
\displaystyle t\mapsto|t|^{q-2}t
for
\displaystyle t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one can estimate
\displaystyle J_{1}
similarly as the homonym term in [
34
, Theorem A.1] to get

{}J_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{K}(x)(\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)-\mathcal{A}u(x,y))(T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(x))-T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y)))d\nu\geq 0.

(7.16)

Let us now set

\displaystyle\omega=\operatorname{supp}\phi_{K},\omega^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{2n}\backslash\left(\omega^{c}\times\omega^{c}\right),

and as
\displaystyle\phi_{K}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, for every
\displaystyle\eta>0
, there exists a compact set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(\eta)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that

{}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.17)

Then, applying Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\backslash\mathcal{K}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu\\
\leq\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{q-1}{q}}\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))|^{q}|\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
\leq\mu\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{q-1}{q}}\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{array}

(7.18)

If
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, note
Lemma

7.1
(c)
, the condition
Eq.

7.9
on
\displaystyle\theta
and choose
\displaystyle m=\frac{p+\delta-1}{p-\beta}>1
in
Lemma

2.13
, to obtain

{}\frac{|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\leq C\frac{\left|u_{\epsilon}(x)^{\frac{\delta+p-1}{p-\beta}}-u_{\epsilon}(y)^{\frac{\delta+p-1}{p-\beta}}\right|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}\quad\text{ for almost all }x,y\in\omega^{\prime}.

(7.19)

Note
Lemma

7.2
and
Eq.

7.19
, so that both for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
and
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, we have from
Eq.

7.18
, for some
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(\eta)

{}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\backslash\mathcal{K}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu\leq C\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{A22}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.20)

For
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, let us set
\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x,y)=T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)).
We show that the functions
\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}
have uniformly absolutely continuous integrals. Let
\displaystyle E\subset\mathcal{K}
be an arbitrary measurable set. Then, proceeding analogously to
Eqs.

7.18
,
7.19
and
7.20
, for some positive constant
\displaystyle C
, independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
, we have

{}\left|\int_{E}g_{\epsilon}(x,y)d\nu\right|\leq C\left(\int_{E}\frac{|\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

(7.21)

Thus,
\displaystyle\int_{E}g_{\epsilon}(x,y)d\nu\rightarrow 0
uniformly on
\displaystyle\epsilon
if
\displaystyle|E|\rightarrow 0
. Also
\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
pointwise in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Hence, by Vitali’s convergence theorem, it follows that there exists a
\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
\displaystyle\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
, it holds

{}\int_{\mathcal{K}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.22)

Hence from
Eqs.

7.20
and
7.22
, it holds

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}J_{2}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu=0.

(7.23)

Recalling
Remark

7.3
, we can proceed similarly like
\displaystyle J_{2}
and end with

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}J_{3}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y))\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)-\phi_{K}(y))d\nu=0.

(7.24)

Now set
\displaystyle v_{\epsilon}=\phi_{K}T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))
. Noting
Remark

7.3
, by a similar estimate like
Eq.

7.19
, we have for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, for every
\displaystyle\eta>0
, there exists a compact set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(\eta)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that

{}\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|u(x)^{\frac{p+\delta-1}{p-\beta}}-u(y)^{\frac{p+\delta-1}{p-\beta}}|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.25)

Eq.

7.25
clearly holds for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
as in that case
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega)
. Using
Eq.

7.25
and the uniform boundedness of
\displaystyle v_{\epsilon}
in
\displaystyle W^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega)
, we get for both the cases
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
and
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1

{}\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(v_{\epsilon}(x)-v_{\epsilon}(y))d\nu\leq\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{q-1}{q}}\left(\int_{\omega^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{K}}\frac{|v_{\epsilon}(x)-v_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.26)

Finally, proceeding analogously to
Eq.

7.21
,
Eq.

7.22
for any given
\displaystyle\eta>0
, there exists
\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}
such that, if
\displaystyle\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we get

{}\int_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(v_{\epsilon}(x)-v_{\epsilon}(y))d\nu\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

(7.27)

Merging
Eqs.

7.26
and
7.27
one has

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}J_{4}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi_{K}(x)T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(x))-\phi_{K}(y)T_{\nu}((u_{\epsilon}-u)(y)))d\nu=0.

(7.28)

Now, employing the estimates
Eqs.

7.16
,
7.23
,
7.24
and
7.28
in
Eq.

7.15
we obtain

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}J\geq 0.

(7.29)

Estimate for
\displaystyle R
:
Recalling that
\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\phi_{K}=\omega
, and
\displaystyle f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
by
Lemma

7.1
, there exists a constant
\displaystyle C(\omega)>0
, independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
, such that
\displaystyle u\geq C(\omega)>0
in
\displaystyle\omega
. Hence, we have

{}R=\int_{\Omega}\frac{f_{\epsilon}}{(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{\delta}}\psi_{\epsilon}dx\leq\frac{\|f\|_{L^{1}(\omega)}}{C(\omega)^{\delta}}\mu.

(7.30)

Therefore, for every fixed
\displaystyle\mu>0
, using the estimates
Eqs.

7.14
,
7.29
and
7.30
in
Eq.

7.10
, we obtain

{}\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{K}(A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})-A(x,\nabla u))\cdot\nabla T_{\mu}((u_{\epsilon}-u))dx\leq C\mu,

(7.31)

for some constant
\displaystyle C=C(\omega,\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\omega)})>0
. Let us define the function
\displaystyle e_{\epsilon}(x)=\langle A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})-A(x,\nabla u),\nabla(u_{\epsilon}-u)\rangle

and note by
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
we have
\displaystyle e_{\epsilon}\geq 0
in
\displaystyle\Omega
. We divide the compact set
\displaystyle K
by

E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}=\{x\in K:|(u_{\epsilon}-u)(x)|\leq\mu\},F_{\epsilon}^{\mu}=\{x\in K:|(u_{\epsilon}-u)(x)|>\mu\}.

Let
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
be fixed. Then, from Hölder’s inequality,

{}\int_{K}e_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}dx=\int_{E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}}e_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}dx+\int_{F_{\epsilon}^{\mu}}e_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}dx\leq\left(\int_{E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}}e_{\epsilon}dx\right)^{\gamma}|E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}|^{1-\gamma}+\left(\int_{F_{\epsilon}^{\mu}}e_{\epsilon}dx\right)^{\gamma}|F_{\epsilon}^{\mu}|^{1-\gamma}.

(7.32)

Now, since
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}(K)
, the sequence
\displaystyle\{e_{\epsilon}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle L^{1}(K)
(by
Eq.

2.6
). Furthermore,
\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}|F_{\epsilon}^{\mu}|=0
. Hence, from
Eqs.

7.31
and
7.32
, we have

{}\lim\sup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{K}e_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}dx\leq\lim\sup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\int_{E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}}e_{\epsilon}dx\right)^{\gamma}|E_{\epsilon}^{\mu}|^{1-\gamma}\leq(C\mu)^{\gamma}|\Omega|^{1-\gamma}.

(7.33)

Letting
\displaystyle\mu\rightarrow 0
in
Eq.

7.33
, the sequence
\displaystyle\{e_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\}
converges to
\displaystyle 0
strongly in
\displaystyle L^{1}(K)
. Therefore, using a sequence of compact sets
\displaystyle K
, up to a subsequence, one gets
\displaystyle e_{\epsilon}(x)\rightarrow 0\text{ almost everywhere in }\Omega,
which along with
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
gives
\displaystyle\nabla u_{n}(x)\rightarrow\nabla u(x)\text{ for almost every }x\in\Omega
.
∎

Theorem 7.5
.

If
\displaystyle\beta\in[0,p)
, then
\displaystyle u
given in
Remark

7.3
is a weak solution to
Eq.

7.1
in the sense of
Definition

2.24
.

Proof.

First let
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
. Then by
Lemma

7.2
the sequence
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Hence by
Eq.

2.6
,
\displaystyle A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
. Therefore, using
Lemma

7.4
and the continuity of
\displaystyle A
in the second variable, for every
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx=\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx.

(7.34)

Since
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
, by
Lemma

2.4
,
\displaystyle\frac{|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q-2}(u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(y))}{|x-y|^{\frac{n+sq}{q^{\prime}}}}\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),
is uniformly bounded and also note

\displaystyle\frac{\phi(x)-\phi(y)}{|x-y|^{\frac{n+sq}{q}}}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Denoting
\displaystyle d\mu=\frac{dxdy}{|x-y|^{n+qs}}
, by weak convergence, we thus have for
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}g(x,y)=|g(x)-g(y)|^{q-2}(g(x)-g(y))
,

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu.

(7.35)

By
Lemma

7.1
,
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\geq C(\omega)>0
on supp
\displaystyle\phi=\omega
for some constant
\displaystyle C(\omega)>0
, independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Therefore, for every
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

\left|{f_{\epsilon}}{(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}}\phi\right|\leq{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\omega)}}{C(\omega)^{-\delta}}|\phi|\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

By the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we have

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}{f_{\epsilon}}{(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}}\phi\,dx=\int_{\Omega}{f}{u^{-\delta}}\phi\,dx.

(7.36)

Combining
Eqs.

7.34
,
7.35
and
7.36
we conclude for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
. Note that
\displaystyle u
satisfies the boundary condition in the sense of
Definition

2.23
. For the case
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, by
Lemma

7.2
the sequence
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{p+\delta-1}{p-\beta}}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Moreover, the sequence
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
is uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Hence
\displaystyle u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Now following the lines of the proof for the above case, for every
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we obtain

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u_{\epsilon})\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx=\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx,\quad\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Omega}{f_{\epsilon}}{(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}}\phi\,dx=\int_{\Omega}{f}{u^{-\delta}}\phi\,dx.

(7.37)

For the nonlocal part, following the same arguments as in [
19
, Theorem 3.6], for every
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

{}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u_{\epsilon}(x,y)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu.

(7.38)

We get the desired result by merging
Eqs.

7.37
and
7.38
. Note that
\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle\theta\geq 1
implies
\displaystyle u
satisfies the boundary behavior in the sense of
Definition

2.23
(see for instance [
29
, Theorem 2.10]). Also note that we have used [
24
, Section 2.5] at every step where required.
∎

7.1
Uniqueness: Proof of
Theorem

2.28

We prove the result for the general equation as given in
Eq.

7.1
.

Step 1
Let
\displaystyle V=W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
with the norm
\displaystyle\|u\|=\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
and let
\displaystyle V^{\prime}
be its dual. Define for all
\displaystyle m>0
, the truncated function
\displaystyle g_{m}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

g_{m}(t):=\begin{cases}\min\{t^{-\delta},m\}&\text{ if }t>0\\
m&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Consider the non-empty closed and convex set

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}:=\{\phi\in V:0\leq\phi\leq v\text{ a.e. in }\Omega\},
where
\displaystyle v
is the given supersolution to
Eq.

2.13
(or
Eq.

7.1
). Under the assumptions on
\displaystyle f
and applying
Theorem

2.9
one can define the operator
\displaystyle J_{m}:V\rightarrow V^{\prime}
for every
\displaystyle u,\psi\in V
by

\langle J_{m}(u),\psi\rangle:=\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy-\int_{\Omega}fg_{m}(u)\psi\,dx.

First, we observe that
\displaystyle J_{m}
is well-defined. For this, similarly like
Lemma

7.1
, we use
Eq.

2.6
, and Hölder inequality for the first two terms and for the third term see by Hardy inequality that

{}\left|\int_{\Omega}fg_{m}(u)\psi\,dx\right|\leq c\int_{\Omega}d^{-\beta}|\psi|dx=c\int_{\Omega}d^{1-\beta}\frac{|\psi|}{d}dx\leq C\left(\int_{\Omega}d^{\frac{(1-\beta)p}{p-1}}dx\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla|\psi||^{p}dx)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty,

(7.39)

for all
\displaystyle\psi\in V
provided
\displaystyle{(1-\beta)p}/{(p-1)}>-1
i.e.
\displaystyle\beta<2-{1}/{p}
. Now for any
\displaystyle u\in V
and
\displaystyle\theta\in(0,1)
, to be chosen

{}\int_{\Omega}d^{-\beta}u\,dx\leq\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{|u|}{d}\right)^{p}\right)^{\frac{1-\theta}{p}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|u|^{r}\right)^{\frac{\theta}{r}}\left(\int_{\Omega}d^{(1-\beta-\theta)l}\right)^{\frac{1}{l}}\leq C\|u\|^{1-\theta}\left(\int_{\Omega}|u|^{r}\right)^{\frac{\theta}{r}}\left(\int_{\Omega}d^{(1-\beta-\theta)l}\right)^{\frac{1}{l}},

(7.40)

where
\displaystyle r<p^{*}
, if
\displaystyle p<n,\frac{1-\theta}{p}+\frac{\theta}{r}+\frac{1}{l}=1
and in the last inequality, we used Hardy inequality. Here finiteness requires
\displaystyle(1-\beta-\theta)l>-1
, which is equivalent to
\displaystyle\theta<\frac{2pr-pr\beta-r}{pr-r+p}
. Due to the fact that
\displaystyle\beta<2-\frac{1}{p}
(note also that
\displaystyle p>1>\theta
) and by
Eq.

7.40
, it is easy to deduce similarly like
Lemma

7.1
that
\displaystyle J_{m}
is coercive. Finally noting

\left|f(x)g_{m}(u_{k})\psi(x)-f(x)g(u)\psi(x)\right|\leq 2m|f||\psi|,

for every sequence
\displaystyle\{u_{k}\}
in
\displaystyle V
converging to
\displaystyle u
in the norm of
\displaystyle V
, and using
Eq.

7.39
, one can follow the same arguments as in
Lemma

7.1
, to get
\displaystyle J_{m}
is demicontinuous. Also, by
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
, it is easy to get that
\displaystyle J_{m}
strictly monotone (also see
Lemma

7.1
). As a consequence of
Theorem

2.12
, there exists a unique
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{K}
such that for every
\displaystyle\psi\in z+(W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\cap L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega))
with
\displaystyle\psi\in\mathbb{K}
, one has

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla z)\cdot\nabla(\psi-z)dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}z(x,y)((\psi-z)(x)-(\psi-z)(y))d\mu\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)(\psi-z)dx,\end{array}

(7.41)

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}u(x,y)=|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y)),\,d\mu=\frac{dxdy}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}
and
\displaystyle L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is the set of all bounded functions with compact support in
\displaystyle\Omega
. Let us now consider a real valued function
\displaystyle g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\displaystyle 0\leq g\leq 1,g\equiv 1
in
\displaystyle[-1,1]
and
\displaystyle g\equiv 0
in
\displaystyle(-\infty,-2]\cup[2,\infty)
. Define the function
\displaystyle\phi_{h}:=g\left(\frac{z}{h}\right)\phi
and
\displaystyle\phi_{h,t}:=\min\left\{z+t\phi_{h},v\right\}
with
\displaystyle h\geq 1
and
\displaystyle t>0
for a given non-negative
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Clearly,
\displaystyle\phi_{h,t}\in z+(W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\cap L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega))
. Then by
Eq.

7.41
,

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla z)\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}z(x,y)((\phi_{h,t}-z)(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z)(y))d\mu\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx,\end{array}

(7.42)

Define,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}I:&=&c\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z)|^{2}(|\nabla\phi_{h,t}|+|\nabla z|)^{p-2}dx\\
&&+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{(|\phi_{h,t}(x)-\phi_{h,t}(y)|+|z(x)-z(y)|^{q-2}((\phi_{h,t}-z)(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z)(y))^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy,\end{array}

where
\displaystyle c
is given by
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
. By
Eq.

7.42
, we now have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}I&\leq&\int_{\Omega}(A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})-A(x,\nabla z))\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)-\mathcal{A}z(x,y))((\phi_{h,t}-z)(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z)(y))d\mu\\
&\leq&\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)((\phi_{h,t}-z)(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z)(y))d\mu\\
&&-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx.\end{array}

Therefore,

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\quad I-\int_{\Omega}f(x)(g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})-g_{m}(z))(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx\leq\int_{\Omega}(A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx\\
\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)((\phi_{h,t}-z)(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z)(y))d\mu-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})(\phi_{h,t}-z)dx\\
=\left(\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h(x,y)dxdy-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx\right)\\
\quad+t\left(\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla\phi_{h}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)(\phi_{h}(x)-\phi_{h}(y))d\mu-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})\phi_{h}dx\right)\end{array}

(7.43)

where
\displaystyle h(x,y)=\frac{\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)((\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}.

Set
\displaystyle\Omega=S_{v}\cup S_{v}^{c}
, where
\displaystyle S_{v}:=\{x\in\Omega:\phi_{h,t}(x)=v(x)\}
and
\displaystyle S_{v}^{c}:=\Omega\backslash S_{v}
. Observe that
\displaystyle A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})=A(x,\nabla v)\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})=0
on
\displaystyle S_{v}^{c}
and
\displaystyle A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})=A(x,\nabla v)\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})
on
\displaystyle S_{v}
. Let us denote by
\displaystyle h_{v}(x,y)=\frac{\mathcal{A}v(x,y)((\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})(x)-(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}.
Now following the exact arguments as in the proof of [
19
, Lemma 4.1], we obtain

\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx=\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla v)\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx,\quad\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}h(x,y)dxdy=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}h_{v}(x,y)dxdy.

Noting the above property along with the fact that
\displaystyle v
is a weak supersolution of
Eq.

7.1
, we choose
\displaystyle(z+t\phi_{h}-\phi_{h,t})\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\cap L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
as a test function and using the definition of
\displaystyle g_{m}
and
\displaystyle\phi_{h,t}
,
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}h(x,y)dxdy-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx\\
=\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla v)\cdot\nabla(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}h_{v}(x,y)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})(\phi_{h,t}-z-t\phi_{h})dx\leq 0.\end{array}

Using the above fact in
Eq.

7.43
with the observation that
\displaystyle I\geq 0
and
\displaystyle\phi_{h,t}-z\leq t\phi_{h}
, we have

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla\phi_{h,t})\cdot\nabla\phi_{h}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}\phi_{h,t}(x,y)(\phi_{h}(x)-\phi_{h}(y))d\mu-\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})\phi_{h}dx\\
\geq-\int_{\Omega}f(x)|g_{m}(\phi_{h,t})-g_{m}(z)|\phi_{h}dx.\end{array}

(7.44)

Note now
\displaystyle z\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
and recall the definition of
\displaystyle\phi_{h,t}
, and use the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, letting
\displaystyle t\rightarrow 0
in
Eq.

7.44
, to obtain

\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla z)\cdot\nabla\phi_{h}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}z(x,y)(\phi_{h}(x)-\phi_{h}(y))d\mu\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)\phi_{h}dx.

Finally, passing the limit as
\displaystyle h\rightarrow\infty
in the above inequality, one gets

{}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla z)\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}z(x,y)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))d\mu\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)\phi\,dx,

(7.45)

for every non-negative
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

Step 2
Let
\displaystyle m>0
and
\displaystyle\epsilon=2m^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}
. Since
\displaystyle u\leq 0
on
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
, for
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{K}
, we have
\displaystyle(u-z-\epsilon)^{+}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. For
\displaystyle\eta>0
, by standard density arguments, it follows from
Eq.

7.45
that

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla z)\cdot\nabla T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}z(x,y)(T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})(x)-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g_{m}(z)T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx.\end{array}

(7.46)

Since
\displaystyle(u-z-\epsilon)^{+}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
,
\displaystyle\exists
a sequence
\displaystyle\phi_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\displaystyle\phi_{k}\rightarrow(u-z-\epsilon)^{+}
strongly in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. We define
\displaystyle\psi_{k,\eta}:=T_{\eta}(\min\{(u-z-\epsilon)^{+},\phi_{k}^{+}\})\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
Since
\displaystyle u
is a weak subsolution of
Eq.

7.1
, we have

\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\psi_{k,\eta}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(\psi_{k,\eta}(x)-\psi_{k,\eta}(y))d\mu\leq\int_{\Omega}f(x)u^{-\delta}\psi_{k,\eta}dx,

Consequently, using dominated convergence theorem and Fatou’s lemma, we can pass the limit as
\displaystyle k\rightarrow\infty
to get

{}\begin{array}[]{c}\int_{\Omega}A(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})(x)-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}f(x)u^{-\delta}T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx.\end{array}

(7.47)

Following the proof of [
19
, Theorem 4.2] we have

\mathcal{A}u(x,y)(T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x))-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x)))=\mathcal{A}u(x,y)((u-z)(x)-(u-z)(y))H(x,y),

\mathcal{A}z(x,y)(T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x))-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x)))=\mathcal{A}z(x,y)((u-z)(x)-(u-z)(y))H(x,y)

with

H(x,y):=\frac{T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x))-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(y))}{(u-z)(x)-(u-z)(y)},

where
\displaystyle(u-z)(x)-(u-z)(y)\neq 0
. Subtracting
Eq.

7.46
from
Eq.

7.47
and then, using the above two displays along with the non-negativity of
\displaystyle H(x,y)
and
Lemma

2.14
(or
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
), we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\quad C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})|^{2}(|\nabla u|+|\nabla z|)^{p-2}dx\\
\quad+C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{(|u(x)-u(y)|+|z(x)-z(y)|)^{q-2}((u-z)(x)-(u-z)(y))^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}H(x,y)dxdy\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}(A(x,\nabla u)-A(x,\nabla z))\cdot\nabla T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx\\
\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{A}u(x,y)-\mathcal{A}z(x,y))(T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(x))-T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+}(y)))d\mu\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\leq\int_{\Omega}f(x)({u^{-\delta}}-g_{m}(z))T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}f(x)(g_{m}(u)-g_{m}(z))T_{\eta}((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})dx\leq 0.\end{array}

In the final estimate above, we have used
\displaystyle\epsilon>m^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}
, the definition of
\displaystyle g_{m}
and the fact that
\displaystyle u\geq\epsilon
in the support of
\displaystyle(u-z-\epsilon)^{+}
. Therefore, using the nonnegativity of the integrand, letting
\displaystyle\eta\rightarrow\infty
, the above estimate yields

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla((u-z-\epsilon)^{+})|^{2}(|\nabla u|+|\nabla z|)^{p-2}dx=0.

As a consequence,
\displaystyle u\leq z+2m^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}\leq v+2m^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}
. Letting
\displaystyle m\rightarrow\infty
, we get
\displaystyle u\leq v
in
\displaystyle\Omega
.

7.2
Existence: Proof of
Theorem

2.27

In view of
Theorem

7.5
and
Remark

7.3
, we deduced the existence of a weak solution to
Eq.

2.13
in the sense of
Definition

2.24
. Further for any
\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
, there exists
\displaystyle C_{\Omega^{\prime}}>0
such that
\displaystyle u(x)\geq C_{\Omega^{\prime}}d(x)>0
in
\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}
. Now note that such a solution of
Eq.

2.13
is indeed a local weak solution in the sense of
Definition

2.16
, which easily follows via density arguments. Using now
Theorem

2.19
(or
Theorem

2.17
), it only remains to verify that
\displaystyle u\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{\beta,\delta}}
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{d_{\beta,\delta}}
is defined in
Definition

2.26
.

Case 1

\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
with
\displaystyle\frac{p-\beta}{p+\delta-1}\in(0,s)

Denote
\displaystyle\tau=\frac{p-\beta}{p+\delta-1}
. Clearly
\displaystyle\tau\in(0,1)
. We have assumed
\displaystyle\tau\in(0,s)
. Let
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
be a constant to be specified later. Define the function
\displaystyle{v}_{\epsilon}(x)=\eta\underline{w}_{\rho}(x)
, where
\displaystyle\rho>0
is such that

\underline{w}_{\rho}(x)=\begin{cases}((d_{e}(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{+})^{\tau}-\epsilon&\text{ if }x\in\Omega\cup\left(\Omega^{c}\right)_{\rho},\\
-\epsilon&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where

d_{e}(x)=\begin{cases}\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)&\text{ if }x\in\Omega;\\
-\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)&\text{ if }x\in(\Omega^{c})_{\rho};\\
-\rho&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and
\displaystyle(\Omega^{c})_{\rho}=\left\{x\in\Omega^{c}:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)<\rho\right\}
. Choose
\displaystyle\rho
small enough so that
\displaystyle\nabla d\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})
and there exists
\displaystyle M>0
such that
\displaystyle|\Delta d|\leq M
in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
. Therefore, for
\displaystyle\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})
with
\displaystyle\psi\geq 0
, and applying [
2
, Theorem 3.3], we obtain upon relabeling
\displaystyle\rho
if needed, for
\displaystyle\epsilon
sufficiently small

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|v_{\epsilon}(x)-v_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q-2}(v_{\epsilon}(x)-v_{\epsilon}(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy&\leq&C\eta^{q-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau(q-1)-sq}\psi\,dx\\
&=&C\eta^{q-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-s)(q-1)-s}\psi\,dx\\
&\leq&C\eta^{q-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,dx,\end{array}

(7.48)

where
\displaystyle C>0
is independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
, while for the local term we have
\displaystyle\nabla v_{\epsilon}=\eta\tau(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau-1}\nabla d\text{ in }\Omega_{\rho}.
As
\displaystyle|\nabla d|=1
,

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}-\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}\psi\,dx&=&(\tau\eta)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\nabla d\cdot\nabla\psi\,dx\\
&=&(\tau\eta)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\left[-\Delta d(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\psi+(p-1)(1-\tau)(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,\right]dx\\
&\leq&(\tau\eta)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\left[M(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\psi+(p-1)(1-\tau)(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,\right]dx\\
&\leq&(\tau\eta)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,dx.\end{array}

(7.49)

Merging
Eqs.

7.48
and
7.49
we get

\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(-\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v_{\epsilon})\psi\,dx\leq C(\eta^{p-1}+\eta^{q-1})\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau-\beta}\psi\,dx.

Using
Eq.

2.14
and definition of
\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}
, we deduce that

{}-\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v_{\epsilon}\leq C(\eta^{p-1}+\eta^{q-1})(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}f_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ weakly in }\Omega_{\rho}.

(7.50)

Next, we observe that
\displaystyle(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}=(\eta(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}+(1-\eta)\epsilon)^{-\delta}
for
\displaystyle x\in\Omega_{\rho}
and distinguish the following cases:

(1) If
\displaystyle\eta(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}\geq(1-\eta)\epsilon
for
\displaystyle x\in\Omega_{\rho}
, then

(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\geq(2\eta)^{-\delta}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}.

Thus, from
Eq.

7.50
for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\eta>0
(independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
), we can infer that:

-\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v_{\epsilon}\leq C(\eta^{p-1}+\eta^{q-1})(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}f_{\epsilon}\leq(2\eta)^{-\delta}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}f_{\epsilon}\leq(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}f_{\epsilon}.

(2) If
\displaystyle\eta(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}\leq(1-\eta)\epsilon
for
\displaystyle x\in\Omega_{\rho}
, we have

(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\geq 2^{-\delta}(1-\eta)^{-\delta}\epsilon^{-\delta}.

We choose
\displaystyle\eta>0
sufficiently small and independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
such that

-\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v_{\epsilon}\leq C(\eta^{p-1}+\eta^{q-1})(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}f_{\epsilon}\leq(\eta^{p-1}+\eta^{q-1})\epsilon^{-\delta}f_{\epsilon}\leq 2^{-\delta}(1-\eta)^{-\delta}\epsilon^{-\delta}f_{\epsilon}\leq(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}f_{\epsilon}.

In both cases, we choose
\displaystyle\eta>0
sufficiently small and independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
, such that

-\Delta_{p}v_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v_{\epsilon}\leq(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}f_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ weakly in }\Omega_{\rho}

As
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\geq c
independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
in
\displaystyle\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}
, we choose
\displaystyle\eta>0
small enough and independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
such that the following inequality holds:

v_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\eta(\operatorname{diam}(\Omega)+1)^{\tau}\leq C_{\rho}\leq u_{1}(x)\leq u_{\epsilon}(x)\quad\text{ in }\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}.

Now
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
. Note by [
2
, Theorem 3.3] that
\displaystyle v_{\epsilon}\in\overline{W}^{s,q}(\Omega_{\rho})
where

\overline{W}^{s,q}(\Omega)=\left\{u\in L^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\exists K\text{ s.t. }\Omega\subset\subset K,\|u\|_{W^{s,q}(K)}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)|^{q-1}}{(1+|x|)^{n+sq}}dx<\infty\right\}.

Therefore by weak comparison principle, see for instance [
1
, Proposition 4.1] (also see the way in
Lemma

7.1
where the monotonicity of
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}\}
was shown),
\displaystyle v_{\epsilon}(x)\leq u_{\epsilon}(x)
in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
i.e.

{}\eta((d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}-\epsilon)\leq u_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

(7.51)

Next, we will prove the upper bound for
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
, for this consider
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}=\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho}
, where

\bar{w}_{\rho}(x)=\begin{cases}((d_{e}(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{+})^{\tau}&\text{ if }x\in\Omega\cup\left(\Omega^{c}\right)_{\rho},\\
0&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

and
\displaystyle\Gamma\geq 1
is a constant to be specified later. Proceeding as
Eq.

7.49
, for
\displaystyle\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})
with
\displaystyle\psi\geq 0
, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{rcl}-\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}\psi\,dx&=&(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\left[-\Delta d(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\psi+(p-1)(1-\tau)(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,\right]dx\\
&\geq&(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}\left[-M(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\psi+(p-1)(1-\tau)(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,\right]dx.\end{array}

(7.52)

On a similar note as before, we have used [
2
, Theorem 3.3] to get

{}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|w_{\epsilon}(x)-w_{\epsilon}(y)|^{q-2}(w_{\epsilon}(x)-w_{\epsilon}(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\geq C\Gamma^{q-1}\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-s)(q-1)-s}\psi\,dx\geq 0.

(7.53)

Merging
Eq.

7.52

Eq.

7.53
and if necessary by reducing
\displaystyle\rho
further, we may assume that there exists
\displaystyle C>0
such that

(p-1)(1-\tau)(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}-M(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)}\geq C(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\text{ in }\Omega_{\rho}.

Therefore,

\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}\psi+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}w_{\epsilon})\psi\,dx\geq C(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}\\
\int_{\Omega_{\rho}}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{(\tau-1)(p-1)-1}\psi\,dx.

Noting that
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\in\overline{W}^{s,q}(\Omega_{\rho})
, by [
2
, Theorem 3.3] and taking into account
Eq.

2.14
, we obtain

{}(-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}+(\Delta)^{s}_{q}w_{\epsilon})\geq C_{1}(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}{f_{\epsilon}(x)}\geq C_{1}(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}\Gamma^{\delta}w_{\epsilon}^{-\delta}{f_{\epsilon}(x)}\quad\text{ weakly in }\Omega_{\rho}.

(7.54)

By using the lower estimate of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
by
\displaystyle v_{\epsilon}
, we obtain

f_{\epsilon}(x)(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\leq f(x)(v_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\leq f(x)\eta^{-\delta}d^{-\tau\delta}.

Since
\displaystyle f\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we observe that
\displaystyle f(x)\eta^{-\delta}d^{-\tau\delta}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Next, we claim that
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
is bounded, independently of
\displaystyle\epsilon
, in
\displaystyle\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}
. Let
\displaystyle\{B_{\rho/4}(x_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
be a finite covering of
\displaystyle\overline{\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}}
such that
\displaystyle\overline{\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}B_{\rho/4}(x_{i})\subset\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho/2}\subset\subset\Omega.
Therefore, by
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
estimate of
Theorem

3.4
, we get

{}\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho/4}(x_{i}))}\leq C\left(\fint_{B_{\rho/2}(x_{i})}u_{\varepsilon}^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}+T_{q-1}(u_{\epsilon};x_{i},\frac{\rho}{4})^{(q-1)/(p-1)}+C+\|f_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{\rho/2})}^{1/(p-1)},

(7.55)

where
\displaystyle C=C(n,p,q,s)>0
is a constant. We see that the last term of
Eq.

7.55
is independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
. For the other terms, we have from
Lemma

7.2
that
\displaystyle\{u_{\epsilon}^{\theta}\}_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle\theta\geq 1
. Therefore,

\fint_{B_{\rho/2}(x_{i})}u_{\varepsilon}^{p}dx\leq C(1+\|u_{\epsilon}^{\theta}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)})\leq C(1+\|u_{\epsilon}^{\theta}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)})\leq C.

Noting that
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}=0
in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega
, we obtain using Hölder inequality

T_{q-1}(u_{\epsilon};x_{i},\frac{\rho}{4})^{q-1}\leq C\rho^{sq}\int_{\Omega\backslash B_{\rho/4}(x_{i})}\frac{|u_{\epsilon}(x)|^{q-1}}{\rho^{n+sq}}dx\leq C\rho^{-n}\|u_{\varepsilon}^{\theta}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{\frac{q-1}{\theta}}\leq C.

Hence,
Eq.

7.55
implies that the sequence
\displaystyle\{\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho})}\}_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
\displaystyle\epsilon
, say
\displaystyle\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho})}\leq K
. Now, we choose
\displaystyle\Gamma
sufficiently large independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle C_{1}(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}\Gamma^{\delta}\geq 1
in
Eq.

7.54
and

w_{\epsilon}=\Gamma(d+\epsilon^{1/\tau})^{\tau}\geq\Gamma d(x)^{\tau}\geq\rho^{\tau}\Gamma\geq K\geq u_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}.

Then, by comparison principle in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
and the above inequality, we get

{}u_{\epsilon}\leq\Gamma(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

(7.56)

Taking
\displaystyle\epsilon\to 0
in
Eqs.

7.56
and
7.51
, we get the desired bound.

Case 2

\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
with
\displaystyle\frac{p-\beta}{p+\delta-1}\in[s,1)
and
\displaystyle\beta\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p+\delta-1)

The lower bound is already proved in
Lemma

7.1
and see also
Remark

7.3
. To show the upper bound, we take
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}=\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho}
. Proceeding similarly as
Eqs.

7.52
and
7.54
and using [
35
, Lemma 3.12] one gets for some constant
\displaystyle C

-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}w_{\epsilon}\geq C(\Gamma\tau)^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau-\beta}+\Gamma^{q-1}h\quad\text{ weakly in }\Omega_{\rho},

for some
\displaystyle h\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})
and
\displaystyle h
is independent of
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Note that

{}\Gamma^{q-1}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})}\leq\frac{C}{2}\Gamma^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau-\beta},

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\epsilon
and
\displaystyle\rho
which may depend on
\displaystyle h
also. Therefore by
Eq.

2.14
it holds

{}-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}w_{\epsilon}\geq C\Gamma^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau-\beta}-\Gamma^{q-1}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\rho})}\geq\frac{C}{2}C_{1}\Gamma^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}f_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }\Omega_{\rho}.

(7.57)

Furthermore, in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
, we have

\Gamma^{p-1}\frac{C}{2}C_{1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{-\delta\tau}\geq(\Gamma(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau})^{-\delta}\geq(\Gamma(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau}})^{\tau}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}=(\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}=(w_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta},

provided
\displaystyle\Gamma^{p-1+\delta}{C}C_{1}\geq 2
. Thus, from
Eq.

7.57
, we obtain

-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}w_{\epsilon}\geq f_{\epsilon}(w_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\quad\text{ weakly in }\Omega_{\rho}.

On account of
Eq.

2.14
and the lower bound of
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
inside
\displaystyle\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}
, we note that

f_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}\leq fu_{\epsilon}^{-\delta}\leq C_{\rho}^{-\delta}d(x)^{-\beta}\leq C_{\rho}^{-\delta}\rho^{-\beta}\quad\text{ in }\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\rho}.

The rest follows proceeding similarly to the previous case.

Case 3

\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
with
\displaystyle\beta=p-q^{\prime}s(p+\delta-1)

The lower bound follows from
Lemma

7.1
. To show the upper bound, similarly to case 2 above, we fix
\displaystyle\Gamma>1
(to be specified later) and choose
\displaystyle\beta_{1}\in(\beta,p)
. Then
\displaystyle\beta_{1}+\delta>1>0
and
\displaystyle\beta_{1}\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta)
. Thus, using [
2
, Theorem 3.3] and [
35
, Lemma 3.12], for
\displaystyle\tau_{1}=\frac{p-\beta_{1}}{p-1+\delta}\in(0,1)
, we have

-\Delta_{p}(\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho})+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}(\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho})\geq\Gamma^{p-1}\frac{C}{2}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\beta_{1}-\delta\tau_{1}}\geq\frac{C}{2}C_{1}\Gamma^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\beta_{1}+\beta}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\delta\tau_{1}}f_{\epsilon},

weakly in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
, for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Furthermore, in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{C}{2}C_{1}\Gamma^{p-1}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\beta_{1}+\beta}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\delta\tau_{1}}&\geq&\frac{C}{2}C_{1}\Gamma^{p-1}(\rho+1)^{-\beta_{1}+\beta}(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{-\delta\tau_{1}}\\
&\geq&(\Gamma(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}})^{\tau_{1}})^{-\delta}\geq(\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho}+\epsilon)^{-\delta},\end{array}

provided
\displaystyle\Gamma^{p-1+\delta}{C}C_{1}(\rho+1)^{-\beta_{1}+\beta}\geq 2
. Then, proceeding similarly to case 1,2 above, we obtain

\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\leq\Gamma\bar{w}_{\rho}
in
\displaystyle\Omega
, for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Passing to the limit as
\displaystyle\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we get
\displaystyle u(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\frac{p-\beta_{1}}{p-1+\delta}}
in
\displaystyle\Omega
, for all
\displaystyle\beta_{1}\in(\beta,p)
.

Case 4

\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1

Note that the lower bound has already been obtained in
Lemma

7.1
. To show the upper bound, we fix
\displaystyle\bar{\beta}>0
and
\displaystyle\sigma>0
such that
\displaystyle\bar{\beta}=(1-\delta)+\sigma(p-1+\delta)>(1-\delta)\geq\beta
We further impose the conditions
\displaystyle\sigma<\frac{p-1+p\delta}{p(p-1+\delta)}
(this implies
\displaystyle\bar{\beta}<2-\frac{1}{p}
) and
\displaystyle\sigma\neq 1-q^{\prime}s
(this implies
\displaystyle\bar{\beta}\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p-1+\delta)
). Thanks to
Eq.

2.14
, we can choose
\displaystyle m\geq 1
such that
\displaystyle f\leq m\tilde{f}
in
\displaystyle\Omega
where
\displaystyle\tilde{f}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
satisfies for some constants
\displaystyle C_{1},C_{2}
the inequality
\displaystyle C_{1}d(x)^{-\bar{\beta}}\leq\tilde{f}(x)\leq C_{2}d(x)^{-\bar{\beta}}
in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho}
. Let
\displaystyle v\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be the solution (as obtained in
Theorem

7.5
) to

-\Delta_{p}v+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v=m\tilde{f}v^{-\delta}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad v>0\text{ in }\Omega,\quad v=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega.

By choice of
\displaystyle m
, we see that
\displaystyle u
is a sub-solution to the above problem. Since
\displaystyle\bar{\beta}<2-\frac{1}{p}
, by applying the weak comparison principle as in
Theorem

2.28
, and by case 2 above, we get

u(x)\leq v(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{\frac{p-\bar{\beta}}{p+\delta-1}}=\Gamma d(x)^{1-\sigma}\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

Therefore, for any
\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}\in(0,1)
, we can choose
\displaystyle\sigma\in(0,1-\bar{\sigma})
satisfying all the assumptions above. This completes the proof of the theorem.

7.3
Optimal Sobolev regularity: Proof of
Theorem

2.29

Define
\displaystyle\tau=\frac{(p-\beta)}{(p+\delta-1)}
, when
\displaystyle\beta\neq p-q^{\prime}s(p+\delta-1)
. For
\displaystyle\beta+\delta>1
, let
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
be the weak solution of
Eq.

7.3
with
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\Delta_{p}+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}
. Then, using the boundary behavior of the approximating sequence
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}
as obtained in
Theorem

2.27
and taking
\displaystyle\phi=u_{\epsilon}
as a test function in the weak formulation of
Eq.

7.3
, we obtain

\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}^{p}\leq\int_{\Omega}f_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{1-\delta}dx\leq C_{1}\int_{\Omega}d^{(1-\delta)\tau-\beta}(x)dx\leq C,

if
\displaystyle(1-\delta)(p-\beta)>(\beta-1)(p+\delta-1)\Leftrightarrow p(\delta-1)+\beta p<(p+\delta-1)\Leftrightarrow\Lambda<1
.
Similarly, by taking
\displaystyle\phi=u_{\epsilon}^{p(\theta-1)+1}
as a test function, we obtain for
\displaystyle\theta>\Lambda>1

\|u^{\theta}_{\epsilon}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}^{p}\leq C\int_{\Omega}f_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{(\theta-1)(p-1)+\theta-\delta}dx\leq C\int_{\Omega}d^{(\theta p-(p-1+\delta))\tau-\beta}(x)dx\leq C.

Now, by passing limits
\displaystyle\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we get the solution
\displaystyle u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
if
\displaystyle\Lambda<1
and
\displaystyle u^{\theta}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
if
\displaystyle\theta>\Lambda\geq 1
.

The only if statement for the case
\displaystyle p<\beta+s(p+\delta-1)
follows from the Hardy inequality and the boundary behavior of the weak solution. Precisely, if
\displaystyle\Lambda\geq 1
, then
\displaystyle u\notin W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Indeed, we have

\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}^{p}\geq C\int_{\Omega}\left|\frac{u(x)}{d(x)}\right|^{p}dx\geq C\int_{\Omega}d^{p(\tau-1)}(x)dx=\infty.

In the same way, if
\displaystyle\theta\in[1,\Lambda]
, then

\|u^{\theta}_{\epsilon}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}^{p}\geq C\int_{\Omega}\left|\frac{u^{\theta}(x)}{d(x)}\right|^{p}dx\geq C\int_{\Omega}d^{p(\theta\tau-1)}(x)dx=\infty,

and we deduce
\displaystyle u^{\theta}\notin W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.

7.4
Hölder regularity: Proof of
Theorem

2.31

For
\displaystyle\beta+\delta\leq 1
, we have
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. For every
\displaystyle\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,
\displaystyle u
satisfies

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy=\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\phi\,dx.

Now for every
\displaystyle\psi\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence of function
\displaystyle 0\leq\psi_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)\rightarrow|\psi|
strongly in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
as
\displaystyle k\rightarrow\infty
and pointwise almost everywhere in
\displaystyle\Omega
. We observe that

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\left|\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\psi\,dx\right|\leq\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}|\psi|dx\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\psi_{k}dx\\
\qquad=\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\psi_{k}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\psi_{k}(x)-\psi_{k}(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)\\
\qquad\leq C(\|u\|^{p-1}+\|u\|^{q-1})\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|\psi_{k}\|\leq C(\|u\|^{p-1}+\|u\|^{q-1})\||\psi|\|\leq C(\|u\|^{p-1}+\|u\|^{q-1})\|\psi\|.\end{array}

(7.58)

for some positive constant
\displaystyle C
. Let
\displaystyle\phi\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
, then there exists a sequence
\displaystyle\{\phi_{k}\}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
which converges to
\displaystyle\phi
strongly in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. We claim that

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\phi_{k}dx=\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\phi\,dx.

Indeed, using
\displaystyle\psi=\phi_{k}-\phi
in
Eq.

7.58
, we obtain

{}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}(\phi_{k}-\phi)dx\right|\leq C(\|u\|^{p-1}+\|u\|^{q-1})\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|\phi_{k}-\phi\|=0.

(7.59)

Again, since
\displaystyle\phi_{k}\rightarrow\phi
strongly in
\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
as
\displaystyle k\rightarrow\infty
, we have

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi_{k}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi_{k}(x)-\phi_{k}(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\right)\\
=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy.\end{array}

(7.60)

Merging
Eqs.

7.59
and
7.60
we get

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{q-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy=\int_{\Omega}fu^{-\delta}\phi\,dx,

for every
\displaystyle\phi\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
. One can now apply
Theorem

2.21
and the boundary behavior of
\displaystyle u
obtained as in
Theorem

2.27
to get the result for
\displaystyle\beta+\delta<1
.

Note now that a solution of
Eq.

2.13
defined in the sense of
Definition

2.24
and as obtained in
Theorem

7.5
is indeed a local weak solution in the sense of
Definition

2.16
, which can be deduced by a standard density argument. Also by
Theorem

2.19
(or
Theorem

2.17
),
\displaystyle u\in C^{0,\eta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for all
\displaystyle\eta\in(0,1)
. Further by
Theorem

2.20
,
\displaystyle u\in C^{1,\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for some
\displaystyle\gamma\in(0,1)
. Also, by the boundary behavior of
\displaystyle u
as obtained in
Theorem

2.27
, the corresponding boundary Hölder regularity follows for the case (ii),(iii),(iv). For the sake of completeness, we include a few details.
In this regard, let
\displaystyle x,y\in\Omega_{\rho}
and suppose without loss of generality
\displaystyle d(x)\geq d(y)
. Now two cases occur:

(I) either
\displaystyle|x-y|<{d(x)}/{64}
, in which case set
\displaystyle 64R=d(x)
and
\displaystyle y\in B_{R}(x)
. Hence, we apply the local Hölder regularity in
\displaystyle B_{R}(x)
and we obtain the regularity.

(II) or
\displaystyle|x-y|\geq{d(x)}/{64}\geq{d(y)}/{64}
in which case we claim that the exponent of global Hölder regularity matches with the power
\displaystyle\zeta
such that
\displaystyle u(x)\leq Cd(x)^{\zeta}
. Indeed for a constant
\displaystyle C>0
large enough, we get

\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\zeta}}\leq\frac{|u(x)|}{|x-y|^{\zeta}}+\frac{|u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\zeta}}\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{u(x)}{d^{\zeta}(x)}+\frac{u(y)}{d^{\zeta}(y)}\right)\leq C,

Thus, we get our claim, and the proof is complete.

7.5
Non-existence: Proof of
Theorem

2.30

On the contrary assume
\displaystyle\exists
a solution
\displaystyle v\in W_{loc}^{1,p}(\Omega)
of
Eq.

2.13
and
\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\geq 1
such that
\displaystyle v^{\gamma_{0}}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. For
\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}<p
, which will be specified later, we choose
\displaystyle f_{\tilde{\beta}}\in L_{loc}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\displaystyle mf_{\tilde{\beta}}(x)\leq f(x)
a.e. in
\displaystyle\Omega
, for some constant
\displaystyle m\in(0,1)
independent of
\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}
, and for some positive constants
\displaystyle c_{3},c_{4}
,

c_{3}d(x)^{-\tilde{\beta}}\leq mf_{\tilde{\beta}}(x)\leq c_{4}d(x)^{-\tilde{\beta}}\text{ in }\Omega_{\rho}.

We now construct a suitable subsolution near the boundary
\displaystyle\partial\Omega
, to arrive at some contradiction. For
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, let
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be the unique solution to the following problem (see
Lemma

7.1
)

{}-\Delta_{p}w_{\epsilon}+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}w_{\epsilon}=mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)\left(w_{\epsilon}+\epsilon\right)^{-\delta},

(7.61)

where
\displaystyle f_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x):=\left(f_{\tilde{\beta}}(x)^{\frac{-1}{\tilde{\beta}}}+\epsilon^{\frac{p-1+\delta}{p-\tilde{\beta}}}\right)^{-\tilde{\beta}}
if
\displaystyle f_{\tilde{\beta}}(x)>0
and 0 otherwise.
Next, we will prove
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\leq u_{0}
in
\displaystyle\Omega
. Note that
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
(by
Theorem

2.21
) and
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}=0
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega
. Hence, for given
\displaystyle\sigma>0
,
\displaystyle\exists\,\varrho>0
such that
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\leq\sigma/2
in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\varrho}
. Moreover,
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma\leq-\sigma/2<0
in
\displaystyle\Omega_{\varrho}
, because
\displaystyle v\geq 0
, and we have
\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}\subset\Omega\backslash\Omega_{\varrho}\subset\subset\Omega.
Therefore,
\displaystyle(w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
and from the weak formulation of
Eq.

7.61
, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla w_{\epsilon}|^{p-2}\nabla w_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{A}w_{\epsilon}(x,y)(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
=\int_{\Omega}\frac{mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)}{(w_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{\delta}}T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}),\end{array}

(7.62)

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}g(x,y)=|g(x)-g(y)|^{q-2}(g(x)-g(y)),\,d\mu=\frac{dxdy}{|x-y|^{n+qs}},\,T_{k}(t):=\min\{t,k\}
for
\displaystyle k>0
and
\displaystyle t\geq 0
. Furthermore, since
\displaystyle v\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,p}(\Omega)
is a weak solution to
Eq.

2.13
, for all
\displaystyle\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

{}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\cdot\nabla\psi+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{A}v(x,y)(\psi(x)-\psi(y))d\mu=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v^{-\delta}\psi.

(7.63)

Let
\displaystyle\{\psi_{j}\}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
\displaystyle\psi_{j}\rightarrow(w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}
in
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Set
\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}:=T_{k}(\min\{(w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+},\psi_{j}^{+}\})
. Then,
\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap L_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, therefore by a standard density argument, from
Eq.

7.63
, we infer that

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\cdot\nabla\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{A}v(x,y)(\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}(x)-\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}(y))d\mu=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v^{-\delta}\tilde{\psi}_{j,k}.

Using the fact that
\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}\subset\subset\Omega
and Fatou lemma, we obtain

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\cdot\nabla T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{A}v(x,y)(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
\geq\int_{\Omega}f(x)v^{-\delta}T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})\geq\int_{\Omega}mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)v^{-\delta}T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+}).\end{array}

(7.64)

Deducting
Eq.

7.64
from
Eq.

7.62
, we infer that

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\quad\int_{\Omega}(|\nabla w_{\epsilon}|^{p-2}\nabla w_{\epsilon}-|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v)\cdot\nabla T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx\\
\quad+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}(\mathcal{A}w_{\epsilon}(x,y)-\mathcal{A}v(x,y))(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)((w_{\epsilon}+\epsilon)^{-\delta}-v^{-\delta})T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx\leq\int_{\Omega}mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)(w_{\epsilon}^{-\delta}-v^{-\delta})T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx.\end{array}

(7.65)

Following the proof of [
19
, Theorem 4.2] we have

\mathcal{A}w_{\epsilon}(x,y)(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))=\mathcal{A}w_{\epsilon}(x,y)((w_{\epsilon}-v)(x)-(w_{\epsilon}-v)(y))H(x,y)

and

\mathcal{A}v(x,y)(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))=\mathcal{A}v(x,y)((w_{\epsilon}-v)(x)-(w_{\epsilon}-v)(y))H(x,y)

with

H(x,y):=\frac{T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y)}{(w_{\epsilon}-v)(x)-(w_{\epsilon}-v)(y)}

where
\displaystyle(w_{\epsilon}-v)(x)-(w_{\epsilon}-v)(y)\neq 0
. Using the above two identities and
Lemma

2.14
and the non-negativity of
\displaystyle H(x,y)
we deduce from
Eq.

7.65

\begin{array}[]{l}\quad C\int_{\Omega}(|\nabla w_{\epsilon}|+|\nabla v|)^{p-2}|\nabla T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})|^{2}\\
\quad+C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{(|w_{\epsilon}(x)-w_{\epsilon}(y)|+|v(x)-v(y)|)^{q-2}((w_{\epsilon}-v)(x)-(w_{\epsilon}-v)(y))^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}H(x,y)dxdy\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}(|\nabla w_{\epsilon}|^{p-2}\nabla w_{\epsilon}-|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v)\cdot\nabla T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx\\
\quad+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}(\mathcal{A}w_{\epsilon}(x,y)-\mathcal{A}v(x,y))(T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(x)-T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})(y))d\mu\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}mf_{\tilde{\beta},\epsilon}(x)(w_{\epsilon}^{-\delta}-v^{-\delta})T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})dx\leq 0,\end{array}

this implies that
\displaystyle T_{k}((w_{\epsilon}-v-\sigma)^{+})=0
a.e. in
\displaystyle\Omega
and since it is true for every
\displaystyle k>0
, we get
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\leq v+\sigma
in
\displaystyle\Omega
. Moreover, the arbitrariness of
\displaystyle\sigma
proves
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}\leq v
in
\displaystyle\Omega
. Owing to the estimates of
\displaystyle w_{\epsilon}
given by
Theorem

2.27
, we have

\displaystyle\eta\left(\left(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{p-1+\delta}{p-\tilde{\beta}}}\right)^{\frac{p-\tilde{\beta}}{p-1+\delta}}-\epsilon\right)\leq w_{\epsilon}(x)\leq v(x)\text{ in }\Omega,
for each
\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}>p-s(\delta+p-1)
. Since
\displaystyle v^{\gamma_{0}}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
, by Hardy inequality, we obtain

\eta^{\gamma_{0}p}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\left(\left(d(x)+\epsilon^{\frac{p-1+\delta}{p-\tilde{\beta}}}\right)^{\frac{p-\tilde{\beta}}{p-1+\delta}}-\epsilon\right)^{\gamma_{0}p}}{d^{p}}\leq C\int_{\Omega}\frac{v^{\gamma_{0}p}}{d^{p}}<\infty,

choosing
\displaystyle p-s(\delta+p-1)<\tilde{\beta}<p
sufficiently close to
\displaystyle p
and taking
\displaystyle\epsilon\downarrow 0
, we obtain the quantity on the left side is not finite, which yields a contraction. This completes the proof of
Theorem

2.30
.

8
Application to a perturbed problem: Proof of
Corollary

2.32

Step 1:
We follow the argument used in
Theorem

2.22
. Let
\displaystyle h
and
\displaystyle\phi
as defined in
Eqs.

6.26
and
6.27
. Let
\displaystyle v\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
be any weak solution to

-\Delta_{p}v+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}v+K(x)((v-1)^{+})^{p-1}=F(x)+{\lambda}{(v^{+})^{-\delta}}\text{ in }\Omega;\quad v=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega,

where
\displaystyle K,F\in L^{n/p}(\Omega)
. We take
\displaystyle\phi((v-1)^{+})\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
as a test function to get

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\Omega}(h^{\prime}((v-1)^{+}))^{p}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v\cdot\nabla(v-1)^{+}dx\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|v(x)-v(y)|^{q-2}(v(x)-v(y))(\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))-\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)))}{|x-y|^{n+sq}}dxdy\\
+\int_{\Omega}K\phi((v-1)^{+})((v-1)^{+})^{p-1}dx=\int_{\Omega}F\phi((v-1)^{+})dx+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\lambda}{(v^{+})^{\delta}}\phi((v-1)^{+})dx.\end{array}

(8.1)

We show that the nonlocal integral (denote it by
\displaystyle J
) is non-negative. For this, the following cases can occur:

(i) If
\displaystyle v(x),v(y)\leq 1
, then
\displaystyle\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))=\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)=0
.

(ii) If
\displaystyle v(x)>1\geq v(y)
, then by the monotonicity of the map
\displaystyle t\mapsto|t|^{q-2}t
,
\displaystyle t\in\mathbb{R}
, and non-negativity of
\displaystyle\phi
, we get

\begin{array}[]{c}|v(x)-v(y)|^{q-2}(v(x)-v(y))(\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))-\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)))=|v(x)-v(y)|^{q-2}(v(x)-v(y))\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))\\
\geq|v(x)-1|^{q-2}(v(x)-1)\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))\\
=|(v-1)^{+}(x)-(v-1)^{+}(y)|^{q-2}((v-1)^{+}(x)-(v-1)^{+}(y))(\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))-\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)))\geq 0.\end{array}

Note that we have used [
16
, Lemma A.2] in the last line. Due to symmetry, the same holds for
\displaystyle v(y)>1\geq v(x)
.

(iii) Finally if
\displaystyle v(x),v(y)>1
, then again by [
16
, Lemma A.2], we have

\begin{array}[]{l}|v(x)-v(y)|^{q-2}(v(x)-v(y))(\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))-\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)))\\
=|(v-1)^{+}(x)-(v-1)^{+}(y)|^{q-2}((v-1)^{+}(x)-(v-1)^{+}(y))(\phi((v-1)^{+}(x))-\phi((v-1)^{+}(y)))\geq 0.\end{array}

Combining above three cases and noting that
\displaystyle(v^{+})^{-\delta}\phi((v-1)^{+})\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle v\geq 1
,
Eq.

8.1
yields

{}\begin{array}[]{l}\int_{\Omega}(h^{\prime}((v-1)^{+}))^{p}|\nabla(v-1)^{+}|^{p}dx+\int_{\Omega}K\phi((v-1)^{+})((v-1)^{+})^{p-1}dx\leq\int_{\Omega}(F+\lambda)\phi((v-1)^{+})dx.\end{array}

Observe that
\displaystyle F+\lambda
is also in
\displaystyle L^{n/p}(\Omega)
, and thus one can follow the same procedure as the proof of
Theorem

2.22
, to get
\displaystyle(v-1)^{+}\in L^{t}(\Omega)
for all
\displaystyle t\in[1,\infty)
.

Step 2
Now let
\displaystyle u\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
be a solution to
Eq.

2.15
. Set
\displaystyle K(x)=b(x,u)/(1+((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}).
Then
\displaystyle K
satisfies

|K(x)|\leq C_{b}\frac{1+|u|^{p^{*}-1}}{1+((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}}=C_{b}\frac{1+|u-1+1|^{p^{*}-1}}{1+((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}}\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\frac{|u-1|^{p^{*}-1}}{1+((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}}\leq C_{1}+C_{2}(1+((u-1)^{+})^{p^{*}-p}),

\displaystyle C_{1},C_{2}
being constants. As
\displaystyle p^{*}-p=p^{2}/(n-p)
and
\displaystyle(u-1)^{+}\in L^{p^{*}}(\Omega)
we deduce that
\displaystyle K\in L^{n/p}(\Omega)
. Then as
\displaystyle u>0
in
\displaystyle\Omega
,
Eq.

2.15
can be written in the form

-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}u=K(x)((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}+K(x)+\lambda(u^{+})^{-\delta}.

By step 1, we deduce that
\displaystyle(u-1)^{+}\in L^{t}(\Omega)
for each
\displaystyle t\in[1,\infty)
. Hence
\displaystyle K(x)((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}+K(x)\in L^{t}(\Omega)
for each
\displaystyle t\in[1,\infty)
, i.e.
\displaystyle K(x)((u-1)^{+})^{p-1}+K(x)\in L^{r}(\Omega)
where
\displaystyle r>n/p
if
\displaystyle p\leq n
and
\displaystyle r=1
if
\displaystyle p>n
. For simplicity we write the equation satisfied by
\displaystyle u
as

-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)^{s}_{q}u=\lambda(u^{+})^{-\delta}+G(x),

where
\displaystyle G(x)\in L^{r}(\Omega)
,
\displaystyle r
is described above. Note that for any
\displaystyle k\geq 1
,
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\lambda(u^{+})^{-\delta}(u-k)^{+}dx\leq\int_{\Omega}\lambda(u-k)^{+}dx
, and then one can follow the steps in [
24
, Proposition 2.1], to conclude
\displaystyle u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

Step 3:
As
\displaystyle u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u=\lambda u^{-\delta}+b(x,u)\leq C_{b}\left(\lambda+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{\delta}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{p^{*}-1+\delta}\right)u^{-\delta}:=\lambda_{*}u^{-\delta}.

Let
\displaystyle v\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be the solution of

\begin{array}[]{c}-\Delta_{p}v+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}v={\lambda_{*}}{v^{-\delta}}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad v>0\text{ in }\Omega,\quad v=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega;\end{array}

as obtained by
Theorem

2.27
(put
\displaystyle\beta=0
in
Eq.

2.14
). Then by weak comparison principle (note that by the same argument, as given in the first case of proof of
Theorem

2.31
, it can be deduced that test functions can be taken from
\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
), and the boundary behavior of
\displaystyle v
, we obtain

\eta d(x)\leq u(x)\leq v(x)\leq\Gamma d(x)^{1-\sigma}\quad\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\text{ for all }\sigma\in(0,1),

for some
\displaystyle\eta,\Gamma>0
, depending on
\displaystyle\lambda,p,\delta
and
\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
. We obtained the lower bound on
\displaystyle u
as
\displaystyle u
is a super-solution to

\begin{array}[]{c}-\Delta_{p}u+(-\Delta)_{q}^{s}u={\lambda}{(u+1)^{-\delta}}\text{ in }\Omega,\quad u>0\text{ in }\Omega,\quad u=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega.\end{array}

Finally we have
\displaystyle\lambda u^{-\delta}+b(x,u)\leq cd(x)^{-\delta}+C_{b}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{p^{*}-1})\leq C_{1}d(x)^{-\delta}
, for some constant
\displaystyle C_{1}
. Therefore using
Theorem

2.21
, we get the
\displaystyle C^{1,\gamma}
regularity of
\displaystyle u
in
\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}
.
